\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

Let \(F_0=0\), \(F_1=F_2=1\), and
\(F_{j+2}=F_{j+1}+F_j\), and put
\(\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2\).  Let \(R(n)\) be the classical Fibonacci
partition function: the number of partitions of \(n\) into distinct
Fibonacci numbers, with the two unit terms identified.  Our main
thermodynamic results concern \(R\) on the standard layers
\[
 \mathcal I_m=[F_{m+1}-1,F_{m+2}-1).
\]
The moment pressure for every real tilt and its first-order transition are
already contained, under an affine normalization, in the golden-ratio
Bernoulli-convolution literature \cite{LauNgai1998,FengOlivier2003,Feng2005}.
We make that normalization explicit and use it as the starting point for
finite-window identities, arithmetic critical refinements, and full
large-deviation lower bounds for \(m^{-1}\log R(n)\) under the uniform law on
\(\mathcal I_m\).

A finite-window Zeckendorf fold gives an exact finite model for this standard
object.  A word
\(\omega\in\{0,1\}^m\) determines the raw sum
\[
  N_m(\omega)=\sum_{j=1}^m\omega_jF_{j+1}.
\]
We consider the finite-window fold obtained by reducing this sum modulo
\(F_{m+2}\) and taking the unique legal Zeckendorf word in the visible
window.  If \(d_m(x)\) is the cardinality of the fiber over a legal word
\(x\), the corresponding finite problem is to identify the multiset
\(\{d_m(x):x\in X_m\}\) and its extremal and moment statistics.

The bridge is exact: the complete fiber list is the list of \(R\) on
\(\mathcal I_m\sqcup\mathcal I_{m+1}\).  A second correspondence is an affine
reindexing of a finite subset-sum spectrum.  Both are finite identities and
do not require a normalization transducer.

\subsection{Main results}

Put \(R(n)=0\) for \(n<0\).  Let \(\sigma_0>2\) be the unique solution of
\[
 \frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}=2;
 \qquad \sigma_0=2.4787507857\ldots.
\]
For \(t>-\sigma_0\), let \(P(t)>0\) be the unique solution of
\[
 \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
 q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}=1,
\]
where \(c(p/q)\) is the odd continued-fraction cost defined before
\Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal}, and put \(P(t)=0\) for
\(t\le-\sigma_0\).  Set
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \mu_C:=\sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
 c(p/q)q^{-\sigma_0},\\
 v_c:=\frac{\displaystyle
 \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
 q^{-\sigma_0}\log q}{\mu_C},
 \qquad
 t_c:=-\sigma_0,\qquad
 \alpha_+:=\frac12\log\varphi.
 \end{gathered}
\]
Define
\[
 Z_m^R(t):=\sum_{n\in\mathcal I_m}R(n)^t.
\]

\begin{theoremA}[One-layer Fibonacci partition thermodynamics]
For every \(t\in\RR\),
\[
 \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log Z_m^R(t)=P(t).
\]
For \(s>\sigma_0\),
\[
 Z_m^R(-s)\longrightarrow
 \frac2{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}-1,
\]
whereas \(Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)\sim2m/\mu_C\).  Under the critical Gibbs law
proportional to \(R(n)^{-\sigma_0}\), the normalized log multiplicity and
generating-layer cost converge jointly to \((v_cU,U)\), with \(U\) uniform on
\([0,1]\).

If \(\mathbf N_m\) is uniform on \(\mathcal I_m\), then
\(m^{-1}\log R(\mathbf N_m)\) satisfies a full large deviation principle
with good rate function
\[
 I(\alpha)=\sup_{t\in\RR}\{t\alpha-P(t)+\log\varphi\}.
\]
Its effective domain is \([0,\alpha_+]\), and
\(I(\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha\) on \([0,v_c]\).
\end{theoremA}

This is \Cref{thm:standard-fibonacci-thermodynamics}.  Its proof uses the
one-layer orbit weights \(2,1\), rather than deducing a statement about one
component from the two-layer fiber mixture.

The same renewal representation retains the normalized generating cost
\(B_m^R=m^{-1}\ell_m^R(\mathbf N_m)\).
\Cref{thm:joint-cost-reward-ldp} proves a full LDP for
\((B_m^R,Y_m^R)\).  Its rate is
\[
 J(\beta,\alpha)=
 (1-\beta)\log\varphi+\beta I(\alpha/\beta)
 \quad(0<\beta\le1,\ 0\le\alpha\le\beta\alpha_+),
\]
with \(J(0,0)=\log\varphi\) and \(J=+\infty\) elsewhere.  In particular,
\[
 J(\beta,\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha
 \qquad(0\le\alpha\le\beta v_c),
\]
so coexistence is an affine face of the joint rate surface rather than an
artifact of one-dimensional projection.  Fixed-cost exponential tilting
supplies the strictly convex part; an exact one- or two-letter low-reward
suffix fills every sufficiently large remaining cost on the affine face.

For the exact finite-window model, put
\(R^\dagger(n)=R(n)+R(n-1)\).  Let
\[
  \widetilde d_m(n)=[z^n]\prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j}),
  \qquad 0\le n<F_{m+2}.
\]
For residues modulo \(F_{m+2}\), define
\[
  \pi_m(n)\equiv F_{m+1}n+F_{m+1}-1\pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\]

\begin{theoremB}[Affine fiber--partition correspondence]
For every \(m\ge1\), the map \(\pi_m\) is a permutation and
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))
  =\widetilde d_m(n)
  =R^\dagger(n)-R^\dagger(n-F_{m+1})
  \qquad(0\le n<F_{m+2}).
\]
\end{theoremB}

This combines \Cref{thm:affine-transfer,thm:partition-difference}.  It gives a
pointwise correspondence, not only an equality of aggregate moments.

There is also a direct interval description:
\[
 d_m^\#(r)=
 \begin{cases}
 R(F_{m+2}+r),&0\le r\le F_{m+1}-2,\\
 R(r),&F_{m+1}-1\le r<F_{m+2}.
 \end{cases}
\]
The known extremal classification of Koc\'abov\'a, Mas\'akov\'a, and
Pelantov\'a can therefore be transferred exactly after an explicit change of
Fibonacci convention.

\begin{theoremC}[Interval identity and largest fibers]
Let \(D_m=\max_{x\in X_m}d_m(x)\).  Then
\[
 D_0=D_1=1,\qquad D_{2k}=F_{k+2}\ (k\ge0),\qquad
 D_{2k+1}=2F_{k+1}\ (k\ge1),
\]
and \(D_m=D_{m-2}+D_{m-4}\) for every \(m\ge6\).  The maximizing residues,
including the exceptional initial cases, are given in
\Cref{thm:unconditional-extremal-fibers}.
\end{theoremC}

For a real tilt \(t\), put
\[
  S_t(m)=\sum_{x\in X_m}d_m(x)^t.
\]
The level sets of \(R\) are sufficiently sparse to produce a frozen
half-line.

\begin{theoremD}[Negative-temperature freezing]
For every real \(t\le-\sigma_0\),
\[
  \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_t(m)=0.
\]
Consequently these pressure limits cannot be represented on all real tilts by
the logarithm of a positive simple isolated eigenvalue of an analytic compact
operator family on a fixed Banach space.
\end{theoremD}

The freezing theorem certifies the half-line \(t\le-\sigma_0\).  The weighted
renewal theorem below supplies the complementary positivity in the candidate
strip and proves that \(-\sigma_0\) is the exact zero-pressure boundary.

The fixed-level information is sharper than the exponential pressure.  Put
\(C_m(k)=\card{\{x\in X_m:d_m(x)=k\}}\), and let \(\Psi(k)\) be Weinstein's
number of generating orbits at level \(k\).

\begin{theoremE}[Local spectrum and critical Gibbs law]
For \(m\ge1\), \(C_m(1)=2\), while for \(k\ge2\),
\[
 C_m(k)\le4\Psi(k),
 \qquad C_m(k)=4\Psi(k)\quad(m\ge2k).
\]
For every \(s>\sigma_0\),
\[
 S_{-s}(m)\longrightarrow
 \mathcal Z(s):=\frac4{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}-2.
\]
This is the exact convergence range for the unnormalized sums.  The function
\(\mathcal Z\) has a simple pole at \(\sigma_0\), and the corresponding
fiber-level Gibbs measures converge in total variation with explicit
critical logarithmic moments.
At the endpoint itself,
\[
 S_{-\sigma_0}(m)\sim\frac{4m}{\mu_C}.
\]
Under the finite-window Gibbs law proportional to
\(d_m(x)^{-\sigma_0}\), the normalized pair consisting of log multiplicity
and generating-layer cost converges in distribution to
\((v_cU,U)\), where \(U\) is uniform on \([0,1]\).
\end{theoremE}

The complete statements are
\Cref{thm:local-fiber-spectrum,thm:spectral-partition-function,thm:critical-gibbs-spectrum,thm:critical-finite-window-gibbs}.
They separate an unnormalized critical phenomenon inside the region where
the exponential pressure vanishes.

\begin{theoremF}[Pressure dictionary and critical refinements]
With
\[
 \kappa=-\left.\frac{d}{ds}\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}
 \right|_{s=\sigma_0},
\]
one has
\[
 \sum_{k\le x}\Psi(k)\sim
 \frac{x^{\sigma_0}}{\sigma_0\kappa}.
\]
Moreover, if
\(\beta_*=\log\varphi/\log(2/\varphi)=2.2705594539\ldots\), then
\[
 \liminf_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_t(m)>0
 \qquad(t>-\beta_*).
\]
For every real \(t\), the limit \(P(t)\) exists.  If
\(t>-\sigma_0\), it is positive and is determined by the
weighted-generator equation
\[
 \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
 q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}=1.
\]
For \(t\le-\sigma_0\), one has \(P(t)=0\).  On
\((-\sigma_0,\infty)\), the pressure is real analytic and strictly convex.
At \(t_c=-\sigma_0\), its left derivative is zero and its right derivative is
a finite, explicitly convergent positive ratio of continued-fraction moments.
\end{theoremF}

The all-real existence, implicit equation, frozen branch, and derivative
corner in this statement are not claimed as new: through
\eqref{eq:erdos-pressure-dictionary} below they recover the spectrum theorem
of Feng \cite[Thm.~1.3]{Feng2005} and the Erd\H{o}s-measure phase transition of
Feng--Olivier \cite[Thms.~2.9--2.10 and App.~A]{FengOlivier2003}.  The
Tauberian orbit law, the totient evaluation of the critical equation, and the
finite-size consequences are the refinements used here.

Let \(\mathbf X_m\) be uniformly distributed on \(X_m\), and put
\[
 Y_m=m^{-1}\log d_m(\mathbf X_m).
\]

\begin{theoremG}[Full large deviations across the freezing corner]
The sequence \((Y_m)\) satisfies a large deviation principle with speed
\(m\) and good rate function
\[
 I(\alpha)=\sup_{t\in\RR}\{t\alpha-P(t)+\log\varphi\}.
\]
Its effective domain is \([0,\alpha_+]\), and
\[
 I(\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha
 \qquad(0\le\alpha\le v_c).
\]
For every \(0<\alpha<v_c\), the probabilities of shrinking neighborhoods
have the exact exponential rate \(-I(\alpha)\).
\end{theoremG}

The complete statement and proof are given in \Cref{thm:full-fiber-ldp}.
The new orbit-filling step fills both parities of every sufficiently large
target layer while preserving the source multiplicity.

For a fixed finite set of primes, \Cref{prop:prime-support-interface} gives
the exact joint generating function for multiplier exponents and generator
cost and transfers it to \(C_m(k)\) with the active boundary weights
\(4,3,1\).  We do not assert a directional coefficient or quenched-velocity
asymptotic: the needed parameter-uniform smooth-point estimate and uniform
integrability for the unbounded cost are not supplied by the cited ACSV
results.  \Cref{rem:prime-support-local-open-interface} records the exact
remaining analytic input.

The proof of \Cref{thm:negative-temperature-freezing} includes a separate
layer-count lemma transferring Weinstein's convention \(f_j=F_{j+1}\).  It
distinguishes the levels \(k=1,2\), endpoints, early layers, and the eventual
equality asserted in Weinstein's theorem.  The compact-operator conclusion is
stated precisely in \Cref{prop:no-analytic-compact-representation} and follows
from analytic perturbation theory.

The pressure corner prevents a direct full application of the
G\"artner--Ellis theorem: its lower-bound conclusion does not cover slopes
strictly between the one-sided derivatives at \(-\sigma_0\).  The
orbit-filling argument in \Cref{thm:full-fiber-ldp} supplies those missing
local lower bounds and proves the full large deviation principle, with an
affine rate on the coexistence interval.

\subsection{Contribution boundary}

For auditability, the dependency boundary is summarized before the detailed
discussion.
\noindent\textbf{Koc\'abov\'a--Mas\'akov\'a--Pelantov\'a.}
Imported: extremal values and maximizing integers for the ordinary Fibonacci
partition function.  Proved here from that input: exact convention and
interval transfer to all maximizing finite-window residues.

\noindent\textbf{Weinstein.}
Imported: the free generator monoid, multiplier product, orbit decomposition,
and eventual layer stabilization.  Proved here from that input: the uniform
early-layer bound, weighted cost renewal, exact finite-window transfer, and
the prime-support coefficient interface.

\noindent\textbf{Erd\H{o}s measure and Bernoulli convolutions.}
Sidorov--Vershik identify normalized finite Fibonacci representation
frequencies with approximants to the Erd\H{o}s measure
\cite{SidorovVershik1998}.  Hu studied its golden-ratio local dimensions
\cite{Hu1997}; Lau--Ngai gave the exact \(L^q\)-spectrum for \(q>0\)
\cite{LauNgai1998}; Feng--Olivier and Feng gave the complete real spectrum and
the negative first-order transition \cite{FengOlivier2003,Feng2005}.  These
are imported results, not merely related background.

If \(\mu\) denotes the golden-ratio Bernoulli convolution and
\(\tau_\mu(t)\) uses the convention
\(\tau_\mu(t)=\lim \log\sum_I\mu(I)^t/\log|I|\), then the level masses are
\(2^{-m}\) times the finite Fibonacci representation counts.  Since the net
scale is \(\varphi^{-m}\), the exact exponential-rate dictionary is
\begin{equation}
 P(t)=t\log2-\tau_\mu(t)\log\varphi.
 \label{eq:erdos-pressure-dictionary}
\end{equation}
Thus the all-real pressure, its positive implicit branch, the negative frozen
branch, the first-order corner, and the generic Legendre multifractal
description are prior results.  The present paper adds the exact
finite-window/fiber correspondences, evaluates Feng's implicit critical
condition as \(\zeta(\sigma_0-1)/\zeta(\sigma_0)=2\), obtains the critical
linear constants and uniform coexistence law, and proves the stated
finite-layer nonexposed-slope lower bounds.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.25\textwidth}p{0.31\textwidth}p{0.34\textwidth}}
\toprule
Object here & Prior result and normalization & Increment established here\\
\midrule
Layer pressure \(P(t)\) & Lau--Ngai for \(t>0\); Feng for all
\(t\in\mathbb R\); \eqref{eq:erdos-pressure-dictionary} & Exact layer/window
translation and renewal coordinates\\
Frozen corner & Feng--Olivier and Feng's negative-\(q\) first-order phase
transition & Totient/zeta evaluation of the critical point and one-sided
moment formula\\
Critical law & Weak-Gibbs phase-transition framework & Constants
\(2/\mu_C\), \(4/\mu_C\), and the uniform finite-layer coexistence limit\\
Rate function & Prior \(L^q\)/Legendre multifractal formalism & Uniform-layer
LDP with explicit orbit-filling lower bounds at nonexposed slopes\\
Finite counts & Sidorov--Vershik's Erd\H{o}s approximants & Affine fold,
partition-difference, two-layer, and maximizing-residue identities\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent\textbf{Kesseb\"ohmer--Stratmann; Fiala--Kleban--\"Ozl\"uk.}
Imported: Stern--Brocot/Knauf denominator pressure.  It supplies an alternate
derivation of the already known pressure formula in the renewal coordinates
needed for the finite-size arguments.

\noindent\textbf{Feller; Wiener--Ikehara; G\"artner--Ellis.}
Imported: classical renewal, Tauberian, and exposed-slope large-deviation
tools.  Proved here from those inputs: the linear critical finite-size law,
uniform coexistence limit, and orbit-filling lower bounds at nonexposed
slopes.

The residue characterization in \Cref{thm:residue-fibers} is a direct
restatement of the fold definition, and \Cref{thm:fourier-inversion} is the
standard character-orthogonality formula.  The finite-window contributions in
\Cref{sec:residue-affine} are the explicit reversal--complement bijection and
affine residue permutation of \Cref{thm:affine-transfer}, together with the
pointwise fiber formula in \Cref{thm:partition-difference}; the latter follows
by truncating the indexed Fibonacci partition generating function.  The
two-interval fiber identity in \Cref{thm:fiber-partition-interval} is also
specific to the finite-window fold.

The values and maximizing residues in
\Cref{thm:unconditional-extremal-fibers} are not a new extremal classification:
they are the exact convention-and-interval transfer of Theorems~4.7 and~5.3 of
Koc\'abov\'a, Mas\'akov\'a, and Pelantov\'a
\cite{KocabovaMasakovaPelantova2005};
\Cref{cor:extremal-fiber-recurrence} is then an immediate Fibonacci-recurrence
consequence.  The additional ingredient in the negative-tilt argument is the
uniform early-layer estimate proved from Weinstein's orbit formulas.  That
estimate, which strengthens eventual layer stabilization to a bound valid in
every layer, yields \Cref{thm:negative-temperature-freezing};
\Cref{prop:no-analytic-compact-representation} is its analytic-perturbation
consequence.  The local microcanonical limit and the exact unnormalized
partition function in \Cref{sec:local-spectrum} combine this finite-window
two-layer identity with Weinstein's published orbit decomposition.  The
Wiener--Ikehara theorem itself is classical \cite{Korevaar2004};
\Cref{thm:psi-tauberian} is its application to the specific orbit Dirichlet
series, and \Cref{thm:pressure-transition-strip} is a separate finite-window
Jensen and maximum-level estimate.  Weinstein's free-monoid bijection,
multiplier multiplicativity, orbit decomposition, and largest-index formula
are published
  \cite[Prop.~3.3 and Thms.~3.8, 5.1, and 10.3]{Weinstein2016}; none of
  these mechanisms is claimed here.  The Stern--Brocot/Knauf denominator
  pressure is likewise published.  The weighted length renewal in
  \Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal} and its exact finite-window transfer
  are additional steps here; the resulting pressure is the
  Bernoulli-convolution spectrum under \eqref{eq:erdos-pressure-dictionary},
  not a new pressure theorem.
The one-layer pressure, critical Gibbs law, and LDP in
\Cref{thm:standard-fibonacci-thermodynamics} are consequences for the
standard function \(R\) itself.  Their lower-bound proof uses the one-layer
orbit weights directly and does not infer componentwise behavior from the
two-layer fiber mixture.
The standard large-deviation upper bound and the lower bounds at exposed
slopes are separated in \Cref{thm:full-fiber-ldp} from the new finite-window
orbit-filling argument, which supplies the missing lower bounds at the
nonexposed coexistence slopes.
The marked critical renewal identity is a bivariate restatement of
\Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal}, whereas its conditional consequences
are additional finite-window results.  The arithmetic renewal theorem and
Blackwell's renewal theorem are classical inputs and are not reproved; the
linear critical finite-size asymptotic and uniform coexistence law are their
finite-window transfers through the exact four-branch identity.

The separately compiled supplement keeps the conditional arithmetic and
integer-moment consequences outside the main theorem spine.  It labels the
moment constants as transfers of Sanna's theorem and the governing quadratic
cubic as already present in work of Chow and Jones \cite{ChowJones2024}.

\subsection{Related work}

The uniqueness theorem underlying the legal expansion is due to Zeckendorf
\cite{Zeckendorf1972}; Lekkerkerker's work provides classical background on
the same numeration system \cite{Lekkerkerker1952}.  Berstel developed the
normalization and automata viewpoint for Fibonacci representations
\cite{Berstel2001}.  The representation-counting function itself has been
studied through recurrence formulas, partition identities, extremal bounds,
and automata by Carlitz \cite{Carlitz1968}, Robbins \cite{Robbins1996},
Bicknell-Johnson and Fielder \cite{BicknellJohnsonFielder1999}, Stockmeyer
\cite{Stockmeyer2008}, Chow and Slattery \cite{ChowSlattery2021}, and Shallit
\cite{Shallit2021}.  Weinstein's orbit and level-set analysis
\cite{Weinstein2016} and the exact extremal classification of Koc\'abov\'a,
Mas\'akov\'a, and Pelantov\'a \cite{KocabovaMasakovaPelantova2005} are the
specific inputs to \Cref{sec:extremal-ldp}.  Sanna proved the power-sum
asymptotics used here and the endpoint law
\(\lim_{q\to\infty}\lambda_q^{1/q}=\varphi^{1/2}\)
\cite[Thms.~1.1 and~1.2]{Sanna2025}.  Zhou gives a broader treatment of
representation functions for related numeration systems and, in particular,
resolves the logarithmic-average conjecture of Chow and Slattery
\cite[Cor.~1.5]{Zhou2023}.
Chow and Jones obtained the same cubic that appears in the quadratic
finite-window recurrence in their analysis of the variance of the partition
function \cite{ChowJones2024}.
The probabilistic version of these finite counts is the Erd\H{o}s measure:
Sidorov and Vershik identify its level masses as normalized Fibonacci
representation frequencies \cite{SidorovVershik1998}.  Hu's local-dimension
analysis \cite{Hu1997}, Lau and Ngai's exact positive-\(q\) spectrum
\cite{LauNgai1998}, Feng and Olivier's weak-Gibbs phase-transition theorem
\cite{FengOlivier2003}, and Feng's explicit all-real golden-ratio formula
\cite{Feng2005} are the direct priority line for the pressure in this paper.
The Stern--Brocot pressure and its Knauf-model realization are taken from
Kesseb\"ohmer--Stratmann and Fiala--Kleban--\"Ozl\"uk
\cite{KessebohmerStratmann2007,FialaKlebanOzluk2003}.  The earlier
Stern--Brocot multifractal theorem of Kesseb\"ohmer and Stratmann
\cite{KessebohmerStratmann2004} concerns interval growth rates, rather than
Fibonacci partition multiplicities or the finite-window uniform law.  The
critical continued-fraction moment uses the fixed-denominator mean theorem of Panov and
Liehl, as recorded in the quantitative modern treatment of
Aistleitner--Borda--Hauke
\cite{Panov1982,Liehl1983,AistleitnerBordaHauke2023}.

\subsection{Organization}

\Cref{sec:preliminaries} defines the finite-window fold and fixes notation.
\Cref{sec:residue-affine} proves the residue, affine, and partition-difference
formulas.  \Cref{sec:extremal-ldp} proves the interval identity, transfers the
extremal classification, establishes the freezing theorem and compact-operator
obstruction, and isolates the large-deviation interface at the pressure
corner.
\Cref{sec:local-spectrum} proves local spectral stabilization, the exact
unnormalized partition function and Gibbs law, the orbit-count Tauberian
asymptotic, the pressure dictionary and arithmetic critical refinements, and
the full large deviation principle across the pressure corner.
\Cref{sec:standard-thermodynamics} proves the one-layer pressure, critical
law, scalar LDP, and joint generation-cost/multiplicity LDP for the classical
Fibonacci partition function itself.
The separately compiled supplement
contains conditional arithmetic refinements, the dyadic ray, and the integer
moment transfer.

 succeeded in 323ms:
\ifdefined\supplementonly
\else
\section{Local fiber spectra and the critical Dirichlet law}
\label{sec:local-spectrum}

The pressure records only exponential growth.  On the proved frozen
half-line, a finer limit survives without exponential normalization.  It is
controlled by the fixed-level stabilization in the preceding section.

For \(m\ge1\) and \(k\ge1\), put
\[
 C_m(k):=\card{\{x\in X_m:d_m(x)=k\}},
 \qquad
 \nu_m:=\sum_{k\ge1}C_m(k)\delta_k.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Local microcanonical spectrum]
\label{thm:local-fiber-spectrum}
For every \(m\ge1\),
\[
 C_m(1)=2.
\]
The two singleton fibers have residues
\(F_{m+1}-1\) and \(F_{m+2}-1\).  For every \(k\ge2\),
\[
 C_m(k)\le4\Psi(k),
 \qquad
 C_m(k)=4\Psi(k)\quad(m\ge2k).
\]
In particular, \(C_m(2)=4\) for \(m\ge4\).  Moreover, the locally finite
measures \(\nu_m\) converge vaguely on the discrete space \(\mathbb N\) to
\[
 \nu_\infty=2\delta_1+4\sum_{k\ge2}\Psi(k)\delta_k.
\]
Equivalently, the masses of every finite subset of \(\mathbb N\) eventually
stabilize.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The fiber--partition interval identity gives, as multisets, the values of
\(R\) on the disjoint consecutive layers \(\mathcal I_m\) and
\(\mathcal I_{m+1}\).  Hence
\begin{equation}
 C_m(k)=N_m(k)+N_{m+1}(k).
 \label{eq:fiber-level-two-layer-count}
\end{equation}
The uniform layer-count lemma gives one value equal to \(1\) in each layer,
the bound \(N_j(k)\le2\Psi(k)\) for \(k\ge2\), and equality for
\(j\ge2k\).  Summing the two layers proves all the asserted counts.  Under
the interval identity, the two level-one points are the left endpoints
\(F_{m+1}-1\) and \(F_{m+2}-1\); inspection of the two cases in
\Cref{thm:fiber-partition-interval} shows that these are also their fiber
residues.  Finally, every compact subset of the discrete space
\(\mathbb N\) is finite, and each of its point masses eventually has the
displayed value.  This is precisely vague convergence.
\end{proof}

Define the unnormalized limiting spectral partition function by
\[
 \mathcal Z(s):=\frac{4}{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}-2
 \qquad(s>\sigma_0).
\]

\begin{theorem}[Exact negative-tilt spectral limit]
\label{thm:spectral-partition-function}
For every \(s>\sigma_0\),
\[
 \lim_{m\to\infty}S_{-s}(m)=\mathcal Z(s)<\infty.
\]
For an arbitrary real tilt \(t\), the exact dichotomy is
\[
 \begin{cases}
 S_t(m)\longrightarrow
 \displaystyle\frac{4}{2-\zeta(-t-1)/\zeta(-t)}-2,&t< -\sigma_0,\\[8pt]
 S_t(m)\longrightarrow+\infty,&t\ge-\sigma_0.
 \end{cases}
\]
Thus \(-\sigma_0\) is the convergence boundary for the unnormalized
partition functions, independently of whether it is the boundary of the
zero-pressure set.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(s>\sigma_0\), \Cref{thm:local-fiber-spectrum} and dominated convergence
give
\[
 S_{-s}(m)
 =\sum_{k\ge1}C_m(k)k^{-s}
 \longrightarrow
 2+4\sum_{k\ge2}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^s}.
\]
The domination follows from \(C_m(k)\le4\Psi(k)\) for \(k\ge2\), and the
majorant is summable by
\eqref{eq:weinstein-level-dirichlet-series}.  Weinstein's Dirichlet identity
then reduces the last expression to \(\mathcal Z(s)\).

Suppose next that \(0<s\le\sigma_0\).  If \(2<s\le\sigma_0\), the geometric
series in \eqref{eq:weinstein-level-dirichlet-series} diverges.  If
\(0<s\le2\), the one-factor generating words give
\(\Psi(k)\ge\phi_{\rm E}(k)\), and the totient Dirichlet series diverges.
Thus \(\sum_{k\ge2}\Psi(k)k^{-s}=+\infty\) throughout this range.  For each
fixed \(K\), \Cref{thm:local-fiber-spectrum} gives, once \(m\ge2K\),
\[
 S_{-s}(m)\ge2+4\sum_{2\le k\le K}\Psi(k)k^{-s}.
\]
Letting \(K\to\infty\) proves divergence for
\(-\sigma_0\le t<0\).  For \(t\ge0\), nonempty fibers satisfy
\(d_m(x)^t\ge1\), and hence
\(S_t(m)\ge\card{X_m}=F_{m+2}\to\infty\).
\end{proof}

The singularity of \(\mathcal Z\) also determines a limiting Gibbs law.  Put
\[
 \kappa:=-\left.\frac{d}{ds}\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}
          \right|_{s=\sigma_0}
 =\sum_{n\ge2}\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(n)\log n}{n^{\sigma_0}}
 =2.589184379946924\ldots.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Critical Gibbs spectrum]
\label{thm:critical-gibbs-spectrum}
As \(s\downarrow\sigma_0\),
\[
 \mathcal Z(s)=\frac{4}{\kappa(s-\sigma_0)}+O(1).
\]
For \(s>\sigma_0\), define
\[
 Q_{m,s}(k):=\frac{C_m(k)k^{-s}}{S_{-s}(m)}.
\]
Then \(Q_{m,s}\) converges in total variation to the probability measure
\[
 Q_s(1)=\frac2{\mathcal Z(s)},
 \qquad
 Q_s(k)=\frac{4\Psi(k)k^{-s}}{\mathcal Z(s)}\quad(k\ge2).
\]
In particular,
\[
 Q_s(1)\sim\frac\kappa2(s-\sigma_0).
\]
If \(K\) has law \(Q_s\), then
\[
 \mathbb E_s[\log K]
 =\frac1{s-\sigma_0}+O(1),
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Var}_s(\log K)
 =\frac1{(s-\sigma_0)^2}+O(1).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The totient series is absolutely differentiable in a neighborhood of
\(\sigma_0>2\), and therefore
\[
 2-\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}
 =\kappa(s-\sigma_0)+O((s-\sigma_0)^2).
\]
The pole expansion and the asymptotic for \(Q_s(1)\) follow.  For fixed
\(s>\sigma_0\), the pointwise convergence and summable domination used in
\Cref{thm:spectral-partition-function} give convergence of the unnormalized
weights in \(\ell^1(\mathbb N)\).  Normalizing therefore gives total
variation convergence.

Absolute differentiation of the limiting Dirichlet series yields
\[
 -\frac{d}{ds}\log\mathcal Z(s)=\mathbb E_s[\log K],
 \qquad
 \frac{d^2}{ds^2}\log\mathcal Z(s)
 =\operatorname{Var}_s(\log K).
\]
Since the denominator defining \(\mathcal Z\) is analytic at \(\sigma_0\)
and has a simple zero there, its Taylor expansion gives
\[
 \log\mathcal Z(s)
 =-\log(s-\sigma_0)+\log(4/\kappa)+O(s-\sigma_0),
\]
where the remainder is analytic.  Differentiating this local analytic
expansion proves the two moment estimates.
\end{proof}

The same pole has a Tauberian consequence for the generating-orbit counts.

\begin{theorem}[Generating-orbit Tauberian law]
\label{thm:psi-tauberian}
As \(x\to\infty\),
\[
 \sum_{k\le x}\Psi(k)
 \sim\frac{x^{\sigma_0}}{\sigma_0\kappa},
 \qquad
 \sum_{k\le x}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^{\sigma_0}}
 \sim\frac1\kappa\log x.
\]
Consequently,
\[
 \left(\frac4\kappa+o(1)\right)\log m
 \le S_{-\sigma_0}(m)
 \le\left(\frac{2\log\varphi}{\kappa}+o(1)\right)m.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Set
\[
 A(s)=\sum_{n\ge2}\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(n)}{n^s}
 =\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}-1.
\]
For \(\Re s>\sigma_0\), Weinstein's identity reads
\[
 1+\sum_{k\ge2}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^s}=\frac1{1-A(s)}.
\]
On the line \(\Re s=\sigma_0\), if \(\tau\ne0\), then
\[
 |A(\sigma_0+i\tau)|<A(\sigma_0)=1.
\]
Indeed, equality in the triangle inequality would force all
\(n^{-i\tau}\) with \(n\ge2\) to have the same phase; since the sum would
have to equal the positive number \(1\), it would in particular force
\(2^{-i\tau}=3^{-i\tau}=1\).  This contradicts
\(\log2/\log3\notin\mathbb Q\).  Thus the Dirichlet series has no other
boundary singularity and has at \(s=\sigma_0\) a simple pole of residue
\(1/\kappa\).  The Wiener--Ikehara theorem
\cite{Korevaar2004} now gives
\[
 \sum_{k\le x}\Psi(k)
 \sim\frac{x^{\sigma_0}}{\sigma_0\kappa}.
\]
Partial summation gives the second asymptotic.

For the critical finite-window sum, stabilization gives
\[
 S_{-\sigma_0}(m)
 \ge2+4\sum_{2\le k\le m/2}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^{\sigma_0}},
\]
which proves the lower bound.  The uniform layer estimate and the absence of
levels above \(D_m\) give
\[
 S_{-\sigma_0}(m)
 \le2+4\sum_{2\le k\le D_m}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^{\sigma_0}}.
\]
Now use the second Tauberian asymptotic and
\(\log D_m\sim\tfrac12m\log\varphi\).
\end{proof}

Finally, the same estimates narrow the possible location of the zero-pressure
boundary.  Write \(p_m(t)=m^{-1}\log S_t(m)\), and set
\[
 \beta_*:=\frac{\log\varphi}{\log(2/\varphi)}
 =2.270559453959664\ldots.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Pressure bounds in the transition strip]
\label{thm:pressure-transition-strip}
For \(0<s<\sigma_0\),
\[
 \max\left\{0,\log\varphi-s\log\frac2\varphi\right\}
 \le\liminf_{m\to\infty}p_m(-s)
\]
and
\[
 \limsup_{m\to\infty}p_m(-s)
 \le\min\left\{\log\varphi,
       \frac{\sigma_0-s}{2}\log\varphi\right\}.
\]
In particular,
\[
 \liminf_{m\to\infty}p_m(t)>0\qquad(t>-\beta_*).
\]
Together with \Cref{thm:negative-temperature-freezing}, this confines any
boundary of the zero-pressure set to
\[
 [-\sigma_0,-\beta_*]
 =[-2.478750785733960\ldots,-2.270559453959664\ldots].
\]
The width of this candidate strip is
\[
 \sigma_0-\beta_*=0.208191331774296\ldots.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under the uniform distribution on \(X_m\), the mean fiber multiplicity is
\(2^m/F_{m+2}\).  Since \(u\mapsto u^{-s}\) is convex, Jensen's inequality
gives
\[
 \frac{S_{-s}(m)}{F_{m+2}}
 \ge\left(\frac{2^m}{F_{m+2}}\right)^{-s}.
\]
After taking logarithms and dividing by \(m\), this gives the nonzero term in
the lower bound; the term zero follows from the two singleton fibers.

For the upper bound, fix \(a>\sigma_0\).  Since all occurring levels satisfy
\(k\le D_m\), \Cref{thm:local-fiber-spectrum} gives
\[
 S_{-s}(m)
 \le2+4D_m^{a-s}\sum_{k\ge2}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^a}.
\]
Using \(m^{-1}\log D_m\to\tfrac12\log\varphi\) and then letting
\(a\downarrow\sigma_0\) proves the second term in the upper bound.  The
first follows directly from \(d_m(x)^{-s}\le1\).  If
\(-\beta_*<t<0\), the lower bound is strictly positive by the definition of
\(\beta_*\); if \(t\ge0\), then \(S_t(m)\ge F_{m+2}\).  This proves the
last assertion without assuming that the pressure limit exists.
\end{proof}

The remaining transition strip admits an exact renewal description.  For a
reduced fraction \(p/q\in(0,1)\), write its unique negative continued fraction
as
\[
 \frac pq=
 \cfrac1{a_1-\cfrac1{a_2-\ddots-\cfrac1{a_r}}},
 \qquad a_i\ge2,
\]
and set
\[
 d(p/q):=\sum_{i=1}^r(a_i-1),
 \qquad c(p/q):=2d(p/q)+1.
\]
For a finite vector \(\mathbf a=(a_1,\ldots,a_r)\), define its negative
continuant by
\[
 \begin{gathered}
  \mathcal D(\varnothing)=1,\qquad \mathcal D(a_1)=a_1,\\
  \mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_r)
  =a_r\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_{r-1})
   -\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_{r-2}).
 \end{gathered}
\]
Thus the denominator of the displayed negative continued fraction is
\(\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_r)\).

For real \(s\), put
\begin{equation}
 b_\ell(s):=
 \sum_{\substack{0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\\c(p/q)=\ell}}q^{-s},
 \qquad
 u_0(s):=1,\qquad
 u_j(s):=\sum_{\ell=1}^j b_\ell(s)u_{j-\ell}(s).
 \label{eq:weighted-renewal-coefficients}
\end{equation}
Every sum defining \(b_\ell(s)\) is finite.  More explicitly,
\begin{equation}
 b_{2d+1}(s)
 =\sum_{r=1}^d
   \sum_{\substack{e_1+\cdots+e_r=d\\e_i\ge1}}
   \mathcal D(e_1+1,\ldots,e_r+1)^{-s},
 \qquad b_{2d}(s)=0.
 \label{eq:weighted-letter-coefficients}
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Weighted generator renewal]
\label{prop:weighted-generator-renewal}
For \(j\ge1\), let
\[
 \Lambda_j(s):=\sum_{n\in\mathcal I_j}R(n)^{-s}.
\]
Then, for every real \(s\),
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda_j(s)
 =1+u_j(s)+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{j-1}u_\ell(s)
 =2\sum_{\ell=0}^{j-1}u_\ell(s)+u_j(s)-1.
 \label{eq:weighted-layer-renewal}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every \(m\ge1\),
\begin{equation}
 S_{-s}(m)
 =4\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(s)+3u_m(s)+u_{m+1}(s)-2.
 \label{eq:weighted-window-renewal}
\end{equation}
Moreover, with formal power series
\[
 B_s(z):=\sum_{\ell\ge1}b_\ell(s)z^\ell,
 \qquad U_s(z):=\sum_{j\ge0}u_j(s)z^j,
\]
one has
\begin{equation}
 U_s(z)=\frac1{1-B_s(z)}.
 \label{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We use the generating-number parameterization in
\cite[Secs.~3--4]{Weinstein2016}, but not its layer-stabilization theorem.
Under Weinstein's free-monoid bijection, a generating number \(g\) corresponds
uniquely to a nonempty word
\[
 w=\frac{p_1}{q_1}\times\cdots\times\frac{p_r}{q_r}
\]
in reduced fractions from \((0,1)\).  Proposition~3.3 and formula~(16) of
that paper give, in the present notation,
\begin{equation}
 R(g)=\prod_{i=1}^r q_i,
 \qquad L(g)=\sum_{i=1}^r c(p_i/q_i).
 \label{eq:generator-weight-length}
\end{equation}
The continued-fraction bijection also gives
\eqref{eq:weighted-letter-coefficients}.  Unique decomposition of a free word
therefore shows that \(u_j(s)\) is exactly the sum of \(R(g)^{-s}\) over all
generating numbers with \(L(g)=j\), and proves
\eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function} as a formal identity.

It remains to count each generating orbit in a fixed layer.  A generating
number belongs to Weinstein's odd first-index sheet, so its initial-block
parameter is \(1\).  The formulas already established in
\eqref{eq:L-a-shift}--\eqref{eq:L-sigma-shift} consequently specialize to
\[
 L([a]g)=L(g)+2a,\qquad
 L([a]\tau g)=L(g)+1+2a,\qquad
 L(\sigma n)=L(n)+1
\]
for \(a\ge0\).  Hence the two branches in Weinstein's first sheet contribute
one point to every layer \(j\ge L(g)\); their sigma images contribute one
further point precisely when \(j>L(g)\).  The action is free and distinct
generating orbits are disjoint.  Since \(\mathcal I_j\) contains exactly one
point of partition multiplicity \(1\) for \(j\ge1\), summing the orbit weights
gives \eqref{eq:weighted-layer-renewal}.  The two-layer identity
\eqref{eq:real-collision-two-layers} then gives
\eqref{eq:weighted-window-renewal}.  Notice that the restriction \(j\ge1\) is
essential in \eqref{eq:weighted-layer-renewal}, because \(\mathcal I_0\) is
empty.
\end{proof}

Let \(\mathfrak P\) denote the Stern--Brocot pressure
\[
 \mathfrak P(\theta)
 :=\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac1d
   \log\sum_{T\in\mathcal T_d}|T|^\theta .
\]
We use the generation convention in which the first interior fraction
\(1/2\) has depth \(d=1\).  The denominator form of the same pressure
\cite[Sec.~2 and Thm.~1.1]{KessebohmerStratmann2007} is
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak P(\theta)=
 \lim_{d\to\infty}\frac1d\log
 \sum_{\substack{0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\\d(p/q)=d}}q^{-2\theta}.
 \label{eq:stern-brocot-denominator-convention}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Continued-fraction/Knauf normalization]
\label{lem:continued-fraction-knauf-normalization}
For \(d\ge1\), write a composition of \(d\) as
\(d=e_1+\cdots+e_r\), and put \(a_i=e_i+1\).  Encode the composition by the
word \(w=w_1\cdots w_{d-1}\in\{L,R\}^{d-1}\) whose \(j\)-th letter is \(R\)
exactly when \(j=e_1+\cdots+e_i\) for some \(i<r\), and is \(L\) otherwise.
With
\[
 L=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 R=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad M_w=w_1\cdots w_{d-1},
\]
the map \((a_1,\ldots,a_r)\mapsto w\) is a bijection, and
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac pq&=[0;a_1,\ldots,a_r]_-=
 \frac{\text{sum of the first row of }M_w}{\|M_w\|_\Sigma},\\
 q&=\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_r)=\|M_w\|_\Sigma.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:negative-cf-word-bijection}
\end{equation}
Thus \(d(p/q)=d\) is Knauf generation \(d\), the corresponding matrix word
has length \(d-1\), and the generator cost is \(c(p/q)=2d+1\).  In particular,
for \(\beta\in\mathbb R\), the even Knauf sum in the convention of
\cite[Sec.~IV]{FialaKlebanOzluk2003} is exactly
\[
 Z^{K,e}_d(2\beta)
 =\sum_{d(p/q)=d}q^{-2\beta}.
\]
There is no \(d=0\) fraction in \((0,1)\); the first four nonempty layers are
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
d&\text{words}&q=\|M_w\|_\Sigma\text{ in word order}&c=2d+1\\ \hline
1&\varnothing&2&3\\
2&L,R&3,\,3&5\\
3&LL,LR,RL,RR&4,\,5,\,5,\,4&7\\
4&\{L,R\}^3&5,\,7,\,8,\,7,\,7,\,8,\,7,\,5&9
\end{array}
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choosing the separator positions of a composition is the standard bijection
between compositions of \(d\) and binary words of length \(d-1\).  Starting
from \(M_\varnothing=I\), simultaneous induction on the word length, using
right multiplication by \(L\) or \(R\), gives the displayed subtractive
Euclidean recursion for the numerator and the negative-continuant recursion
for the denominator.  Explicitly, appending \(L\) replaces the last
composition part \(e_r\) by \(e_r+1\), whereas appending \(R\) replaces it by
\((e_r,1)\); substituting these two operations in
\(\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_r)
=a_r\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_{r-1})
-\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_{r-2})\) agrees with
\(M_{wL}=M_wL\) and \(M_{wR}=M_wR\), respectively.  This proves
\eqref{eq:negative-cf-word-bijection}.
The depth and cost identities follow from the definitions.  The Knauf
identity is then term-by-term: its inverse temperature is \(2\beta\), so its
denominator weight is \(q^{-2\beta}\).  The table also fixes the empty-word
and first-generation conventions explicitly.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[New-denominator layer estimate]
\label{lem:knauf-layer-estimate}
For every fixed real \(s<2\),
\begin{equation}
 a_d(s):=
 \sum_{\substack{0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\\d(p/q)=d}}q^{-s}
 \asymp_s \exp\{d\mathfrak P(s/2)\}.
 \label{eq:knauf-layer-pressure}
\end{equation}
For every fixed \(s\ge2\), one has instead
\[
 \lim_{d\to\infty}\frac1d\log a_d(s)=0.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First suppose \(0<s<2\), and put \(\beta=s/2\).  The even Knauf
partition function \(Z^{K,e}_d(2\beta)\) is exactly \(a_d(s)\): its
states are the new Stern--Brocot denominators at depth \(d\).  In the
notation of Fiala, Kleban, and \"Ozl\"uk, the first-coordinate identity
for their Knauf vector, its Perron--Frobenius iteration, and its spectral
asymptotic are given in
\cite[Sec.~IV, especially Eqs.~(23), (26), and (28)]{FialaKlebanOzluk2003}.
They give
\[
 a_d(s)=c_s\lambda(s/2)^d(1+o(1)),\qquad c_s>0.
\]
Their free-energy identification, together with the denominator formula
for Stern--Brocot pressure
\cite[Thm.~1.1 and the denominator formula in Sec.~2]{KessebohmerStratmann2007},
gives \(\log\lambda(s/2)=\mathfrak P(s/2)\).  This proves
\eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure} in the range \(0<s<2\).  At \(s=0\),
there are exactly \(2^{d-1}\) new denominators and
\(\mathfrak P(0)=\log2\), so the assertion is immediate.

It remains to supply the estimate for \(s<0\), a range not covered by
the positive-temperature Perron--Frobenius statement just cited.  Put
\(t=-s>0\), and let
\[
 L=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 R=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \lVert M\rVert_\Sigma=\sum_{i,j=1}^2M_{ij}.
\]
 By \Cref{lem:continued-fraction-knauf-normalization}, the new denominators at
 depth \(d\) are in bijection with words \(w\in\{L,R\}^{d-1}\), and
\begin{equation}
 q(w)=\lVert M_w\rVert_\Sigma.
 \label{eq:stern-brocot-matrix-denominator}
\end{equation}
Define
\[
 Z_n(t):=\sum_{w\in\{L,R\}^n}\lVert M_w\rVert_\Sigma^t,
 \qquad n\ge0.
\]
For nonnegative matrices \(A,B\),
\(\lVert AB\rVert_\Sigma\le
\lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma\), and hence
\begin{equation}
 Z_{m+n}(t)\le Z_m(t)Z_n(t).
 \label{eq:matrix-partition-submultiplicative}
\end{equation}
For the reverse inequality with one bridging letter, write
\(\alpha=A^T(1,1)^T\) and \(\beta=B(1,1)^T\).  Direct multiplication gives
\[
 \lVert ALB\rVert_\Sigma+\lVert ARB\rVert_\Sigma
 =\lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma
   +\alpha_1\beta_1+\alpha_2\beta_2.
\]
Thus at least one \(C\in\{L,R\}\) satisfies
\(\lVert ACB\rVert_\Sigma\ge
\frac12\lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma\).  Summing over
all pairs of words yields
\begin{equation}
 Z_{m+n+1}(t)\ge2^{-t}Z_m(t)Z_n(t).
 \label{eq:matrix-partition-bridged}
\end{equation}

Let \(\lambda_t=\lim_n Z_n(t)^{1/n}\), whose existence follows from
\eqref{eq:matrix-partition-submultiplicative}.  That inequality gives
\(Z_n(t)\ge\lambda_t^n\).  Iterating
\eqref{eq:matrix-partition-bridged} \(k-1\) times and then letting
\(k\to\infty\) gives
\[
 Z_{k(n+1)-1}(t)\ge2^{-t(k-1)}Z_n(t)^k,
 \qquad
 Z_n(t)\le2^t\lambda_t^{n+1}.
\]
Consequently \(Z_n(t)\asymp_t\lambda_t^n\).  By
\eqref{eq:stern-brocot-matrix-denominator} and the denominator form of
Stern--Brocot pressure cited above,
\(\log\lambda_t=\mathfrak P(-t/2)\).  Since
\(a_d(-t)=Z_{d-1}(t)\), this proves
\eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure} for \(s<0\).

Finally, the logarithmic assertion for \(s\ge2\) is precisely the
denominator formulation of the zero-pressure part of
\cite[Thm.~1.1]{KessebohmerStratmann2007}.
\end{proof}

The following pressure statement is retained because its renewal
normalization is used below.  Its existence, implicit branch, frozen phase,
and nondifferentiable critical point recover the golden-ratio
Bernoulli-convolution results of Feng--Olivier and Feng
\cite{FengOlivier2003,Feng2005}; they are not claimed as new.

\begin{theorem}[Pressure in renewal coordinates]
\label{thm:exact-negative-pressure}
\label{thm:all-real-pressure}
For every \(s\in\mathbb R\), the radius of convergence of \(B_s\) is
\begin{equation}
 R_B(s)=\exp\{-\mathfrak P(s/2)/2\}.
 \label{eq:Bs-radius}
\end{equation}
Thus \(R_B(s)<1\) for \(s<2\), and \(R_B(s)=1\) for \(s\ge2\).
The nonnegative real roots of \(B_s(r)=1\) are classified as follows:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
s<\sigma_0 & \text{a unique root }r_s\in(0,R_B(s)),\\
s=\sigma_0 & \text{the unique root }r_s=1=R_B(s),\\
s>\sigma_0 & \text{no root in }[0,R_B(s)].
\end{array}
\]
The pressure exists for every real tilt and satisfies
\begin{equation}
 P(-s):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_{-s}(m)
 =\begin{cases}
   -\log r_s,&s<\sigma_0,\\
   0,&s\ge\sigma_0.
  \end{cases}
 \label{eq:all-real-pressure}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, for \(t>-\sigma_0\), \(P(t)>0\) is the unique solution of
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{q=2}^{\infty}
 \sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
 q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}=1,
 \label{eq:exact-negative-pressure}
\end{equation}
whereas \(P(t)=0\) for \(t\le-\sigma_0\).  In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \{t\in\mathbb R:P(t)>0\}=(-\sigma_0,\infty).
 \label{eq:exact-zero-pressure-set}
\end{equation}

The restriction of \(P\) to \((-\sigma_0,\infty)\) is real analytic and
strictly convex.  At \(t_c=-\sigma_0\), it has the finite one-sided
derivatives
\begin{equation}
 P'_-(t_c)=0,
 \qquad
 P'_+(t_c)=
 \frac{\displaystyle
  \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
       q^{-\sigma_0}\log q}
 {\displaystyle
  \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
       c(p/q)q^{-\sigma_0}}>0.
 \label{eq:critical-pressure-slopes}
\end{equation}
Consequently, \(P\) is not differentiable at the freezing point.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write \(a_d(s):=b_{2d+1}(s)\).  The negative-continued-fraction
parameterization gives
\[
 a_d(s)=\sum_{\substack{0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\\d(p/q)=d}}q^{-s}.
\]
There are \(2^{d-1}\) fractions in this layer, and their denominators satisfy
\(d+1\le q\le F_{d+2}\).  In particular, every \(B_s\) has positive finite
radius.  The layer is precisely the new-denominator layer of the Knauf model.
 The precise estimate in \Cref{lem:knauf-layer-estimate} gives, for fixed
\(s<2\), \eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure}, and gives
\(d^{-1}\log a_d(s)\to0\) for \(s\ge2\).  Cauchy--Hadamard now proves
\eqref{eq:Bs-radius}: explicitly,
\(B_s(z)=\sum_{d\ge1}a_d(s)z^{2d+1}\), so the cost \(2d+1\) contributes the
factor \(1/2\) in the radius exponent.  Moreover,
\eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure} shows that
the terms of \(B_s\) do not tend to zero on \(|z|=R_B(s)\) when \(s<2\).
At \(s=2\), the positive real boundary also diverges.  For \(s>2\), Tonelli's
theorem gives
\begin{equation}
 B_s(1)=\sum_{q\ge2}\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(q)}{q^s}
 =\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}-1<\infty.
 \label{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one}
\end{equation}

The coefficients are nonnegative and the support contains both \(3\) and
\(5\), so \(B_s\) is strictly increasing on its positive interval of
convergence.  If \(s\le2\), its positive boundary value is infinite; if
\(2<s<\sigma_0\), \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one} is greater than one.
These facts give the unique interior root for \(s<\sigma_0\).  At
\(s=\sigma_0\), the boundary value is one, while it is less than one for
\(s>\sigma_0\).  This proves the asserted real-root classification.  The
triangle inequality also shows that, for \(s<\sigma_0\), the only zero of
\(1-B_s(z)\) in \(|z|\le r_s\) is the simple zero \(r_s\): equality forces
the degree-three and degree-five terms to have the same positive phase, and
\(\gcd(3,5)=1\).

Concatenation of generating words gives
\(u_{m+n}(s)\ge u_m(s)u_n(s)\).  The letters of costs \(3\) and \(5\) imply
\(u_j(s)>0\) for every \(j\ge8\).  The shifted Fekete argument used in the
proof of the strip result therefore applies without any restriction on
\(s\), and yields
\[
 \gamma_s:=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac1j\log u_j(s).
\]
If \(s<\sigma_0\), the formal identity
\(U_s=(1-B_s)^{-1}\), positivity, and the preceding root classification show
that the radius of \(U_s\) is \(r_s\).  At \(s=\sigma_0\), it is one.  If
\(s>\sigma_0\), then \(U_s(1)=(1-B_s(1))^{-1}<\infty\), while
\(u_j(s)\ge b_j(s)\) prevents the radius of \(U_s\) from exceeding
\(R_B(s)=1\).  Hence
\[
 R_U(s)=\begin{cases}r_s,&s<\sigma_0,\\1,&s\ge\sigma_0,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad \gamma_s=-\log R_U(s)\ge0.
\]
For \(H_m(s)=\sum_{j=0}^m u_j(s)\), the exact renewal identity gives
\[
 H_m(s)\le S_{-s}(m)\le4H_{m+1}(s).
\]
Since \(m^{-1}\log H_m(s)\to\gamma_s\), this proves
\eqref{eq:all-real-pressure} and the root equation.

For \(t>-\sigma_0\), put
\[
 \mu_t(p/q):=q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}.
\]
The root equation makes this a probability measure.  The root lies strictly
inside the relevant convergence circle, and
\(\log q\ll d(p/q)\), so the defining series and all its parameter
derivatives converge locally uniformly.  The analytic implicit-function
theorem applies.  With \(X=\log q\) and \(C=c(p/q)\), differentiation gives
\begin{equation}
 P'(t)=\frac{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}X}{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}C},
 \qquad
 P''(t)=
 \frac{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}[(X-P'(t)C)^2]}
      {\mathbb E_{\mu_t}C}.
 \label{eq:pressure-derivatives}
\end{equation}
The fractions \(1/2\) and \(1/3\) have \((C,q)=(3,2)\) and \((5,3)\),
and \(\log2/3\ne\log3/5\).  Thus the variance in
\eqref{eq:pressure-derivatives} is strictly positive.

It remains to justify passage to the critical endpoint.  For the canonical
regular continued fraction \(p/q=[0;a_1,\ldots,a_r]\), with \(a_r>1\),
the conversion between regular and negative continued fractions gives
\[
 d(p/q)=a_1+\cdots+a_r-1,
 \qquad c(p/q)=2(a_1+\cdots+a_r)-1.
\]
The fixed-denominator mean theorem of Panov and Liehl
\cite{Panov1982,Liehl1983,AistleitnerBordaHauke2023} states that
\[
 \frac1{\phi_{\rm E}(q)}
 \sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}(a_1+\cdots+a_r)
 \sim\frac6{\pi^2}(\log q)^2.
\]
Consequently, both the numerator and denominator in
\eqref{eq:critical-pressure-slopes} converge absolutely, since they are
bounded by constant multiples of

artifacts\oracle_referee_A6.md:226:TAMS 并不要求每篇文章解决具名公开问题，但通常要求主结果在其领域中具有明显的结构性推进、较宽的适用范围或足够深的单一核心。现稿在重新归属后存在以下层级问题：
