  543: \begin{equation}
  544:  q(w)=\lVert M_w\rVert_\Sigma.
  545:  \label{eq:stern-brocot-matrix-denominator}
  546: \end{equation}
  547: Define
  548: \[
  549:  Z_n(t):=\sum_{w\in\{L,R\}^n}\lVert M_w\rVert_\Sigma^t,
  550:  \qquad n\ge0.
  551: \]
  552: For nonnegative matrices \(A,B\),
  553: \(\lVert AB\rVert_\Sigma\le
  554: \lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma\), and hence
  555: \begin{equation}
  556:  Z_{m+n}(t)\le Z_m(t)Z_n(t).
  557:  \label{eq:matrix-partition-submultiplicative}
  558: \end{equation}
  559: For the reverse inequality with one bridging letter, write
  560: \(\alpha=A^T(1,1)^T\) and \(\beta=B(1,1)^T\).  Direct multiplication gives
  561: \[
  562:  \lVert ALB\rVert_\Sigma+\lVert ARB\rVert_\Sigma
  563:  =\lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma
  564:    +\alpha_1\beta_1+\alpha_2\beta_2.
  565: \]
  566: Thus at least one \(C\in\{L,R\}\) satisfies
  567: \(\lVert ACB\rVert_\Sigma\ge
  568: \frac12\lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma\).  Summing over
  569: all pairs of words yields
  570: \begin{equation}
  571:  Z_{m+n+1}(t)\ge2^{-t}Z_m(t)Z_n(t).
  572:  \label{eq:matrix-partition-bridged}
  573: \end{equation}
  574: 
  575: Let \(\lambda_t=\lim_n Z_n(t)^{1/n}\), whose existence follows from
  576: \eqref{eq:matrix-partition-submultiplicative}.  That inequality gives
  577: \(Z_n(t)\ge\lambda_t^n\).  Iterating
  578: \eqref{eq:matrix-partition-bridged} \(k-1\) times and then letting
  579: \(k\to\infty\) gives
  580: \[
  581:  Z_{k(n+1)-1}(t)\ge2^{-t(k-1)}Z_n(t)^k,
  582:  \qquad
  583:  Z_n(t)\le2^t\lambda_t^{n+1}.
  584: \]
  585: Consequently \(Z_n(t)\asymp_t\lambda_t^n\).  By
  586: \eqref{eq:stern-brocot-matrix-denominator} and the denominator form of
  587: Stern--Brocot pressure cited above,
  588: \(\log\lambda_t=\mathfrak P(-t/2)\).  Since
  589: \(a_d(-t)=Z_{d-1}(t)\), this proves
  590: \eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure} for \(s<0\).
  591: 
  592: Finally, the logarithmic assertion for \(s\ge2\) is precisely the
  593: denominator formulation of the zero-pressure part of
  594: \cite[Thm.~1.1]{KessebohmerStratmann2007}.
  595: \end{proof}
  596: 
  597: The following pressure statement is retained because its renewal
  598: normalization is used below.  Its existence, implicit branch, frozen phase,
  599: and nondifferentiable critical point recover the golden-ratio
  600: Bernoulli-convolution results of Feng--Olivier and Feng
  601: \cite{FengOlivier2003,Feng2005}; they are not claimed as new.
  602: 
  603: \begin{theorem}[Pressure in renewal coordinates]
  604: \label{thm:exact-negative-pressure}
  605: \label{thm:all-real-pressure}
  606: For every \(s\in\mathbb R\), the radius of convergence of \(B_s\) is
  607: \begin{equation}
  608:  R_B(s)=\exp\{-\mathfrak P(s/2)/2\}.
  609:  \label{eq:Bs-radius}
  610: \end{equation}
  611: Thus \(R_B(s)<1\) for \(s<2\), and \(R_B(s)=1\) for \(s\ge2\).
  612: The nonnegative real roots of \(B_s(r)=1\) are classified as follows:
  613: \[
  614: \begin{array}{c|c}
  615: s<\sigma_0 & \text{a unique root }r_s\in(0,R_B(s)),\\
  616: s=\sigma_0 & \text{the unique root }r_s=1=R_B(s),\\
  617: s>\sigma_0 & \text{no root in }[0,R_B(s)].
  618: \end{array}
  619: \]
  620: The pressure exists for every real tilt and satisfies
  621: \begin{equation}
  622:  P(-s):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_{-s}(m)
  623:  =\begin{cases}
  624:    -\log r_s,&s<\sigma_0,\\
  625:    0,&s\ge\sigma_0.
  626:   \end{cases}
  627:  \label{eq:all-real-pressure}
  628: \end{equation}
  629: Equivalently, for \(t>-\sigma_0\), \(P(t)>0\) is the unique solution of
  630: \begin{equation}
  631:  \sum_{q=2}^{\infty}
  632:  \sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
  633:  q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}=1,
  634:  \label{eq:exact-negative-pressure}
  635: \end{equation}
  636: whereas \(P(t)=0\) for \(t\le-\sigma_0\).  In particular,
  637: \begin{equation}
  638:  \{t\in\mathbb R:P(t)>0\}=(-\sigma_0,\infty).
  639:  \label{eq:exact-zero-pressure-set}
  640: \end{equation}
  641: 
  642: The restriction of \(P\) to \((-\sigma_0,\infty)\) is real analytic and
  643: strictly convex.  At \(t_c=-\sigma_0\), it has the finite one-sided
  644: derivatives
  645: \begin{equation}
  646:  P'_-(t_c)=0,
  647:  \qquad
  648:  P'_+(t_c)=
  649:  \frac{\displaystyle
  650:   \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
  651:        q^{-\sigma_0}\log q}
  652:  {\displaystyle
  653:   \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
  654:        c(p/q)q^{-\sigma_0}}>0.
  655:  \label{eq:critical-pressure-slopes}
  656: \end{equation}
  657: Consequently, \(P\) is not differentiable at the freezing point.
  658: \end{theorem}
  659: 
  660: \begin{proof}
  661: Write \(a_d(s):=b_{2d+1}(s)\).  The negative-continued-fraction
  662: parameterization gives
  663: \[
  664:  a_d(s)=\sum_{\substack{0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\\d(p/q)=d}}q^{-s}.
  665: \]
  666: There are \(2^{d-1}\) fractions in this layer, and their denominators satisfy
  667: \(d+1\le q\le F_{d+2}\).  In particular, every \(B_s\) has positive finite
  668: radius.  The layer is precisely the new-denominator layer of the Knauf model.
  669:  The precise estimate in \Cref{lem:knauf-layer-estimate} gives, for fixed
  670: \(s<2\), \eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure}, and gives
  671: \(d^{-1}\log a_d(s)\to0\) for \(s\ge2\).  Cauchy--Hadamard now proves
  672: \eqref{eq:Bs-radius}: explicitly,
  673: \(B_s(z)=\sum_{d\ge1}a_d(s)z^{2d+1}\), so the cost \(2d+1\) contributes the
  674: factor \(1/2\) in the radius exponent.  Moreover,
  675: \eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure} shows that
  676: the terms of \(B_s\) do not tend to zero on \(|z|=R_B(s)\) when \(s<2\).
  677: At \(s=2\), the positive real boundary also diverges.  For \(s>2\), Tonelli's
  678: theorem gives
  679: \begin{equation}
  680:  B_s(1)=\sum_{q\ge2}\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(q)}{q^s}
  681:  =\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}-1<\infty.
  682:  \label{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one}
  683: \end{equation}
  684: 
  685: The coefficients are nonnegative and the support contains both \(3\) and
  686: \(5\), so \(B_s\) is strictly increasing on its positive interval of
  687: convergence.  If \(s\le2\), its positive boundary value is infinite; if
  688: \(2<s<\sigma_0\), \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one} is greater than one.
  689: These facts give the unique interior root for \(s<\sigma_0\).  At
  690: \(s=\sigma_0\), the boundary value is one, while it is less than one for
  691: \(s>\sigma_0\).  This proves the asserted real-root classification.  The
  692: triangle inequality also shows that, for \(s<\sigma_0\), the only zero of
  693: \(1-B_s(z)\) in \(|z|\le r_s\) is the simple zero \(r_s\): equality forces
  694: the degree-three and degree-five terms to have the same positive phase, and
  695: \(\gcd(3,5)=1\).
  696: 
  697: Concatenation of generating words gives
  698: \(u_{m+n}(s)\ge u_m(s)u_n(s)\).  The letters of costs \(3\) and \(5\) imply
  699: \(u_j(s)>0\) for every \(j\ge8\).  The shifted Fekete argument used in the
  700: proof of the strip result therefore applies without any restriction on
  701: \(s\), and yields
  702: \[
  703:  \gamma_s:=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac1j\log u_j(s).
  704: \]
  705: If \(s<\sigma_0\), the formal identity
  706: \(U_s=(1-B_s)^{-1}\), positivity, and the preceding root classification show
  707: that the radius of \(U_s\) is \(r_s\).  At \(s=\sigma_0\), it is one.  If
  708: \(s>\sigma_0\), then \(U_s(1)=(1-B_s(1))^{-1}<\infty\), while
  709: \(u_j(s)\ge b_j(s)\) prevents the radius of \(U_s\) from exceeding
  710: \(R_B(s)=1\).  Hence
  711: \[
  712:  R_U(s)=\begin{cases}r_s,&s<\sigma_0,\\1,&s\ge\sigma_0,
  713:  \end{cases}
  714:  \qquad \gamma_s=-\log R_U(s)\ge0.
  715: \]
  716: For \(H_m(s)=\sum_{j=0}^m u_j(s)\), the exact renewal identity gives
  717: \[
  718:  H_m(s)\le S_{-s}(m)\le4H_{m+1}(s).
  719: \]
  720: Since \(m^{-1}\log H_m(s)\to\gamma_s\), this proves
  721: \eqref{eq:all-real-pressure} and the root equation.
  722: 
  723: For \(t>-\sigma_0\), put
  724: \[
  725:  \mu_t(p/q):=q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}.
  726: \]
  727: The root equation makes this a probability measure.  The root lies strictly
  728: inside the relevant convergence circle, and
  729: \(\log q\ll d(p/q)\), so the defining series and all its parameter
  730: derivatives converge locally uniformly.  The analytic implicit-function
  731: theorem applies.  With \(X=\log q\) and \(C=c(p/q)\), differentiation gives
  732: \begin{equation}
  733:  P'(t)=\frac{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}X}{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}C},
  734:  \qquad
  735:  P''(t)=
  736:  \frac{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}[(X-P'(t)C)^2]}
  737:       {\mathbb E_{\mu_t}C}.
  738:  \label{eq:pressure-derivatives}
  739: \end{equation}
  740: The fractions \(1/2\) and \(1/3\) have \((C,q)=(3,2)\) and \((5,3)\),
  741: and \(\log2/3\ne\log3/5\).  Thus the variance in
  742: \eqref{eq:pressure-derivatives} is strictly positive.
  743: 
  744: It remains to justify passage to the critical endpoint.  For the canonical
  745: regular continued fraction \(p/q=[0;a_1,\ldots,a_r]\), with \(a_r>1\),
  746: the conversion between regular and negative continued fractions gives
  747: \[
  748:  d(p/q)=a_1+\cdots+a_r-1,
  749:  \qquad c(p/q)=2(a_1+\cdots+a_r)-1.
  750: \]
  751: The fixed-denominator mean theorem of Panov and Liehl
  752: \cite{Panov1982,Liehl1983,AistleitnerBordaHauke2023} states that
  753: \[
  754:  \frac1{\phi_{\rm E}(q)}
  755:  \sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}(a_1+\cdots+a_r)
  756:  \sim\frac6{\pi^2}(\log q)^2.
  757: \]
  758: Consequently, both the numerator and denominator in
  759: \eqref{eq:critical-pressure-slopes} converge absolutely, since they are
  760: bounded by constant multiples of

 succeeded in 361ms:
  760: bounded by constant multiples of
  761: \(\sum q^{1-\sigma_0}\log q\) and
  762: \(\sum q^{1-\sigma_0}(\log q)^2\), respectively.  Dominated convergence in
  763: the first identity of \eqref{eq:pressure-derivatives} gives the stated finite
  764: positive right derivative.  The pressure is identically zero to the left of
  765: \(t_c\), so its left derivative is zero.
  766: \end{proof}
  767: 
  768: \begin{theorem}[Full large deviations across the freezing corner]
  769: \label{thm:full-fiber-ldp}
  770: Let \(\mathbf X_m\) be uniformly distributed on \(X_m\), and set
  771: \[
  772:  \begin{gathered}
  773:  y_m(x):=\frac1m\log d_m(x),\qquad
  774:  Y_m:=y_m(\mathbf X_m),\\
  775:  t_c:=-\sigma_0,\qquad v_c:=P'_+(t_c),
  776:  \qquad \alpha_+:=\frac12\log\varphi.
  777:  \end{gathered}
  778: \]
  779: Then \((Y_m)\) satisfies a full large deviation principle with speed \(m\)
  780: and good rate function
  781: \begin{equation}
  782:  I(\alpha)=\sup_{t\in\mathbb R}
  783:  \{t\alpha-P(t)+\log\varphi\}.
  784:  \label{eq:full-fiber-ldp-rate}
  785: \end{equation}
  786: Its effective domain is \([0,\alpha_+]\).  On the coexistence interval,
  787: \begin{equation}
  788:  I(\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha
  789:  \qquad(0\le\alpha\le v_c).
  790:  \label{eq:frozen-affine-rate}
  791: \end{equation}
  792: More precisely, for every \(0<\alpha<v_c\),
  793: \begin{equation}
  794:  \begin{aligned}
  795:  &\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\liminf_{m\to\infty}
  796:  \frac1m\log\Pr\{|Y_m-\alpha|<\varepsilon\}\\
  797:  &\quad=
  798:  \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\limsup_{m\to\infty}
  799:  \frac1m\log\Pr\{|Y_m-\alpha|<\varepsilon\}\\
  800:  &\quad=-\bigl(\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha\bigr).
  801:  \end{aligned}
  802:  \label{eq:frozen-local-large-deviation}
  803: \end{equation}
  804: \end{theorem}
  805: 
  806: \begin{proof}
  807: For \(\alpha\in\mathbb R\) and \(\varepsilon>0\), write
  808: \[
  809:  A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
  810:  :=\card{\left\{x\in X_m:
  811:  \left|m^{-1}\log d_m(x)-\alpha\right|<\varepsilon\right\}}.
  812: \]
  813: We first establish the lower bound at the nonexposed slopes.  Fix
  814: \(t>t_c\), put \(v=P'(t)>v_c\), and define the tilted law on \(X_n\) by
  815: \[
  816:  \nu_{n,t}(x):=\frac{d_n(x)^t}{S_t(n)}.
  817: \]
  818: For sufficiently small \(s>0\), the exponential Markov inequality and
  819: \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} give
  820: \[
  821:  \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1n
  822:  \log\nu_{n,t}\{y_n\ge v+\delta\}
  823:  \le P(t+s)-P(t)-s(v+\delta)<0.
  824: \]
  825: The corresponding estimate with \(s<0\) gives the same conclusion for
  826: \(y_n\le v-\delta\).  Consequently,
  827: \[
  828:  \sum_{\substack{x\in X_n\\|y_n(x)-v|<\delta}}d_n(x)^t
  829:  =\exp\{n(P(t)+o(1))\}.
  830: \]
  831: Every summand on the left is at most
  832: \(\exp\{n(tv+|t|\delta)\}\), and hence
  833: \begin{equation}
  834:  \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log A_n(v,\delta)
  835:  \ge P(t)-tv-|t|\delta.
  836:  \label{eq:exposed-source-count}
  837: \end{equation}
  838: 
  839: We now transfer these source states to a larger layer without changing their
  840: multiplicities.  The proof of \Cref{thm:fiber-partition-interval} gives the
  841: explicit bijection
  842: \[
  843:  \chi_n(x)=
  844:  \begin{cases}
  845:   F_{n+2}+V_n(x),&0\le V_n(x)\le F_{n+1}-2,\\
  846:   V_n(x),&F_{n+1}-1\le V_n(x)<F_{n+2},
  847:  \end{cases}
  848: \]
  849: from \(X_n\) onto the integer points of
  850: \(\mathcal I_n\sqcup\mathcal I_{n+1}\), with
  851: \(d_n(x)=R(\chi_n(x))\).  Take
  852: \(\delta<v/2\), so their multiplicities exceed two for all sufficiently
  853: large \(n\).  The uniform layer-count lemma shows that each Weinstein
  854: generating orbit supplies at most four source states to these two layers.
  855: The specialized formulas in the proof of
  856: \Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal} give
  857: \[
  858:  L([a]g)=L(g)+2a,\qquad
  859:  L([a]\tau g)=L(g)+1+2a,
  860: \]
  861: and the two sigma branches increase these lengths by one.  Hence every orbit
  862: meeting \(\mathcal I_n\sqcup\mathcal I_{n+1}\) has generator length at most
  863: \(n+1\).
  864: Keeping the generating number of every orbit which occurs therefore gives a
  865: set \(\mathcal G_n\) such that
  866: \begin{equation}
  867:  \card{\mathcal G_n}\ge\frac14 A_n(v,\delta),
  868:  \qquad L(g)\le n+1\quad(g\in\mathcal G_n),
  869:  \label{eq:source-generating-orbits}
  870: \end{equation}
  871: and \(n^{-1}\log R(g)\in(v-\delta,v+\delta)\), since the orbit action
  872: preserves \(R\).  Distinct elements of
  873: \(\mathcal G_n\) generate disjoint orbits.
  874: 
  875: Fix \(0<\alpha<v_c\), set \(\rho=\alpha/v\), and let
  876: \(n_m=\lfloor\rho m\rfloor\).  For sufficiently large \(m\), every
  877: \(g\in\mathcal G_{n_m}\) has \(L(g)<m\).  The generator formulas in the
  878: proof of \Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal} permit the parity-free
  879: definition
  880: \[
  881:  z_m(g):=
  882:  \begin{cases}
  883:  [a]g,&m-L(g)=2a,\\
  884:  [a]\tau g,&m-L(g)=2a+1.
  885:  \end{cases}
  886: \]
  887: They give \(L(z_m(g))=m\) and \(R(z_m(g))=R(g)>1\).  Thus
  888: \(z_m(g)\in\mathcal I_m\), and \(\chi_m^{-1}\) turns these into distinct
  889: states of \(X_m\).  Moreover,
  890: \[
  891:  \left|m^{-1}\log R(z_m(g))-\alpha\right|
  892:  \le \frac{n_m}{m}\delta+\frac vm.
  893: \]
  894: Combining this injection with
  895: \eqref{eq:exposed-source-count}--\eqref{eq:source-generating-orbits} yields,
  896: for every \(\varepsilon>\rho\delta\),
  897: \begin{equation}
  898:  \liminf_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
  899:  \ge \rho\bigl(P(t)-tv-|t|\delta\bigr).
  900:  \label{eq:orbit-filling-lower-bound}
  901: \end{equation}
  902: First let \(\delta\downarrow0\), and then let \(t\downarrow t_c\).  Since
  903: \(P(t)\to0\), \(P'(t)=v\to v_c\), and
  904: \[
  905:  \rho(P(t)-tv)=\alpha\left(\frac{P(t)}v-t\right)
  906:  \longrightarrow-t_c\alpha=\sigma_0\alpha,
  907: \]
  908: we obtain
  909: \begin{equation}
  910:  \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\liminf_{m\to\infty}
  911:  \frac1m\log A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
  912:  \ge\sigma_0\alpha.
  913:  \label{eq:frozen-count-lower}
  914: \end{equation}
  915: 
  916: The matching upper bound uses the critical pressure directly.  On the set
  917: counted by \(A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)\),
  918: \(d_m(x)^{-\sigma_0}\ge
  919: \exp\{-\sigma_0m(\alpha+\varepsilon)\}\).  Therefore
  920: \[
  921:  A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
  922:  e^{-\sigma_0m(\alpha+\varepsilon)}
  923:  \le S_{-\sigma_0}(m)=e^{o(m)},
  924: \]
  925: which, together with \eqref{eq:frozen-count-lower}, proves the corresponding
  926: counting limit.  Since \(\card{X_m}=F_{m+2}\) and
  927: \(m^{-1}\log F_{m+2}\to\log\varphi\), this is
  928: \eqref{eq:frozen-local-large-deviation}.
  929: 
  930: We next identify the convex dual.  If \(0\le\alpha\le v_c\), then
  931: \(P(t)=0\) for \(t\le t_c\), while convexity and the right derivative at
  932: \(t_c\) give \(P(t)\ge v_c(t-t_c)\) for \(t\ge t_c\).  It follows that
  933: \(t\alpha-P(t)\le t_c\alpha\) for every \(t\), with equality at
  934: \(t=t_c\), proving \eqref{eq:frozen-affine-rate}.
  935: 
  936: The exact maximum theorem gives
  937: \[
  938:  \max_{x\in X_m}m^{-1}\log d_m(x)\longrightarrow\alpha_+.
  939: \]
  940: For \(t>0\), the bounds
  941: \(D_m^t\le S_t(m)\le F_{m+2}D_m^t\) imply
  942: \[
  943:  \alpha_+\le\frac{P(t)}t
  944:  \le\alpha_++\frac{\log\varphi}{t}.
  945: \]
  946: By convexity, (P') is increasing and therefore has a limit in
  947: \((0,+\infty]\).  The identity
  948: \[
  949:  \frac{P(t)}t=\frac{P(0)}t+\frac1t\int_0^t P'(u)\,du
  950: \]
  951: and Ces\`aro convergence show that this limit is \(\alpha_+\).  Thus
  952: \(P'(t)\uparrow\alpha_+\) as \(t\to\infty\).  Strict convexity and
  953: analyticity on \((t_c,\infty)\) now show that every
  954: \(\alpha\in(v_c,\alpha_+)\) is exposed by a unique \(t>t_c\).  The tilted
  955: estimate above with \(n=m\) supplies
  956: \begin{equation}
  957:  \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\liminf_{m\to\infty}
  958:  \frac1m\log\Pr\{|Y_m-\alpha|<\varepsilon\}
  959:  \ge P(t)-t\alpha-\log\varphi=-I(\alpha).
  960:  \label{eq:exposed-local-large-deviation}
  961: \end{equation}
  962: 
  963: Finally, the logarithmic moment generating functions under the uniform law
  964: converge on all of \(\mathbb R\) to
  965: \[
  966:  \Lambda(t)=P(t)-\log\varphi.
  967: \]
  968: The variables are eventually supported in a fixed compact interval, so they
  969: are exponentially tight.  The G\"artner--Ellis upper bound
  970: \cite[Theorem~2.3.6(a)]{DemboZeitouni2010} therefore gives the
  971: large-deviation upper bound for all closed sets.
  972: 
  973: It remains to justify the effective domain and endpoint lower bounds.  If
  974: \(\alpha<0\), letting \(t\to-\infty\) in
  975: \eqref{eq:full-fiber-ldp-rate} and using \(P(t)=0\) gives
  976: \(I(\alpha)=+\infty\).  If \(\alpha>\alpha_+\), the limit
  977: \(P(t)/t\to\alpha_+\) gives the same conclusion as \(t\to\infty\).
  978: Conversely, if \(0\le\alpha\le\alpha_+\), then
  979: \(t\alpha-P(t)\) is bounded above on \(( -\infty,t_c]\), on the compact
  980: interval \([t_c,0]\), and on \([0,\infty)\), where
  981: \(P(t)\ge t\alpha_+\).  Thus the effective domain is exactly
  982: \([0,\alpha_+]\).  The rate is a lower-semicontinuous convex function, and
  983: its finiteness on this closed interval implies continuity relative to the
  984: interval, including at the endpoints.
  985: 
  986: The local lower bounds already proved cover the coexistence and exposed
  987: parts of \((0,\alpha_+)\), and points from either side approximate \(v_c\).
  988: At \(0\), the two singleton fibers give the lower bound directly.  At
  989: \(\alpha_+\), continuity of \(I\) and exposed points approaching from below
  990: supply the lower bound for every open neighborhood.  Hence the lower bound
  991: holds for every open set.  The effective domain is compact and \(I\) is
  992: lower semicontinuous, so \(I\) is good and the full large deviation principle
  993: follows.
  994: \end{proof}
  995: 
  996: \subsection{Microcanonical phase separation}
  997: 
  998: The renewal description also retains the generating layer, rather than only
  999: the multiplicity.  Let
 1000: \[
 1001:  \mathcal A:=\{p/q:0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\},
 1002:  \qquad \pi(p/q):=q^{-\sigma _0}.
 1003: \]
 1004: The critical identity \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one} gives
 1005: \(\sum_{\mathcal A}\pi=1\).  Let \((C_i,H_i)_{i\ge1}\) be independent with
 1006: \[
 1007:  \Pr\{(C_i,H_i)=(c(p/q),\log q)\}=\pi(p/q),
 1008: \]
 1009: put \(S_n=\sum_{i=1}^nC_i\), \(T_n=\sum_{i=1}^nH_i\), and define the marked
 1010: renewal measure
 1011: \[
 1012:  \mathcal U:=\sum_{n\ge1}\mathcal L(S_n,T_n).
 1013: \]
 1014: The moment estimates in the proof of \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} show that
 1015: \[
 1016:  \mu_C:=\mathbb E C_1<\infty,
 1017:  \qquad \mu_H:=\mathbb E H_1<\infty,
 1018:  \qquad \frac{\mu_H}{\mu_C}=v_c.
 1019: \]
 1020: 
 1021: We shall use the following marked form of the already proved weighted renewal
 1022: identity.  It is recorded explicitly to retain the generating cost in the
 1023: conditional argument.  For $x\in X_m$ with \(d_m(x)>2\), let \(g_m(x)\) be
 1024: the unique generating number of the Weinstein orbit containing \(\chi_m(x)\).
 1025: Define
 1026: \[
 1027:  K_m(c):=
 1028:  \begin{cases}
 1029:   4,&c\le m-1,\\
 1030:   3,&c=m,\\
 1031:   1,&c=m+1,\\
 1032:   0,&c\ge m+2.
 1033:  \end{cases}
 1034: \]
 1035: For every Borel set \(B\subset\mathbb R\times(\log2,\infty)\),
 1036: \begin{equation}
 1037:  \card{\{x\in X_m:(L(g_m(x)),\log d_m(x))\in B\}}
 1038:  =\int_B K_m(s)e^{\sigma_0t}\,\mathcal U(ds,dt).
 1039:  \label{eq:marked-microcanonical-count}
 1040: \end{equation}
 1041: Indeed, for a generating number \(g\) of cost \(c=L(g)\), the four branches
 1042: and their layer indices are
 1043: \[
 1044:  \begin{array}{c|c}
 1045:  {}[a]g&c+2a\\
 1046:  {}[a]\tau g&c+1+2a\\
 1047:  \sigma([a]g)&c+1+2a\\
 1048:  \sigma([a]\tau g)&c+2+2a.
 1049:  \end{array}
 1050: \]
 1051: The free action makes the middle two points distinct.  Thus this orbit
 1052: contributes zero, one, or two points to \(\mathcal I_j\), according as
 1053: \(j<c\), \(j=c\), or \(j>c\).  Summing over the two disjoint layers
 1054: \(\mathcal I_m\sqcup\mathcal I_{m+1}\) gives exactly \(K_m(c)\).  The two
 1055: unit-multiplicity endpoints correspond to the empty word and are excluded by
 1056: the condition \(t>\log2\).
 1057: 
 1058: Under Weinstein's free-monoid bijection, a nonempty word
 1059: \(w=(p_1/q_1)\times\cdots\times(p_n/q_n)\) has
 1060: \[
 1061:  L(g)=S_n,\qquad \log R(g)=T_n,
 1062:  \qquad \pi(w)=e^{-\sigma_0T_n}.
 1063: \]
 1064: Multiplication of its renewal mass by \(e^{\sigma_0T_n}\), followed by the
 1065: orbit count \(K_m(S_n)\), therefore counts every corresponding finite-window
 1066: state once.  This proves \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count}.
 1067: 
 1068: For the endpoint tilt, define the finite-window Gibbs measure
 1069: \[
 1070:  \widehat Q_m(x):=\frac{d_m(x)^{-\sigma_0}}{S_{-\sigma_0}(m)}
 1071:  \qquad(x\in X_m),
 1072: \]
 1073: and put \(\ell_m(x)=L(g_m(x))\) when \(d_m(x)>2\), while
 1074: \(\ell_m(x)=0\) when \(d_m(x)\le2\).
 1075: 
 1076: \begin{theorem}[Critical finite-size and coexistence law]
 1077: \label{thm:critical-finite-window-gibbs}
 1078: Let
 1079: \[
 1080:  \mu_C=
 1081:  \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
 1082:  c(p/q)q^{-\sigma_0}.
 1083: \]
 1084: Then
 1085: \begin{equation}
 1086:  S_{-\sigma_0}(m)\sim\frac{4m}{\mu_C}.
 1087:  \label{eq:critical-linear-finite-size}
 1088: \end{equation}
 1089: If \(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m\) has law \(\widehat Q_m\) and
 1090: \(U\) is uniform on \([0,1]\), then
 1091: \begin{equation}
 1092:  \left(
 1093:   \frac1m\log d_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m),
 1094:   \frac1m\ell_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)
 1095:  \right)
 1096:  \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\mathrm d}(v_cU,U).
 1097:  \label{eq:critical-uniform-coexistence}
 1098: \end{equation}
 1099: \end{theorem}
 1100: 
 1101: \begin{proof}
 1102: At \(s=\sigma_0\), the coefficients \(b_j(\sigma_0)\) form a probability
 1103: distribution on the positive integers by
 1104: \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one}.  Its span is one because its support
 1105: contains both \(3\) and \(5\), and its mean is \(\mu_C<\infty\).  The
 1106: arithmetic renewal theorem therefore gives
 1107: \[
 1108:  u_j(\sigma_0)\longrightarrow\frac1{\mu_C}.
 1109: \]
 1110: Substitution in the exact identity
 1111: \eqref{eq:weighted-window-renewal}, followed by Ces\`aro summation, proves
 1112: \eqref{eq:critical-linear-finite-size}.
 1113: 
 1114: We use the critical letter law \(\pi(p/q)=q^{-\sigma_0}\) and the marked
 1115: renewal variables \((S_n,T_n)\) defined above
 1116: \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count}.  For every bounded continuous
 1117: function \(f\) on \([0,1]\), the arithmetic renewal limit and a Riemann-sum
 1118: argument give
 1119: \[
 1120:  \frac1m\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(\sigma_0)f(j/m)
 1121:  \longrightarrow\frac1{\mu_C}\int_0^1 f(y)\,dy.
 1122: \]
 1123: The terms at \(j=m,m+1\) are bounded, while
 1124: \(K_m(j)=4\) for \(j\le m-1\).  The total critical weight of the states
 1125: with multiplicity at most two is also bounded uniformly in \(m\).
 1126: Multiplying \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count} by
 1127: \(e^{-\sigma_0t}\), and then using
 1128: \eqref{eq:critical-linear-finite-size}, proves
 1129: \begin{equation}
 1130:  \frac{\ell_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)}m
 1131:  \xrightarrow{\mathrm d}U.
 1132:  \label{eq:critical-uniform-layer}
 1133: \end{equation}
 1134: 
 1135: It remains to retain the reward mark.  The strong law gives
 1136: \(T_n/S_n\to\mu_H/\mu_C=v_c\) almost surely.  For \(\delta>0\), let
 1137: \[
 1138:  A_m=\mathcal U\{0<S\le m+1,\ |T/S-v_c|>\delta\}.
 1139: \]
 1140: Every renewal step has cost at least three.  Hence, on each sample path, the
 1141: number of renewal epochs with \(S_n\le m+1\), divided by \(m\), is bounded;
 1142: the corresponding number of exceptional epochs divided by \(m\) tends to
 1143: zero by the strong law.  Dominated convergence yields \(A_m=o(m)\).
 1144: Since \(K_m\le4\), the marked identity and
 1145: \eqref{eq:critical-linear-finite-size} now imply
 1146: \[
 1147:  \frac1m\log d_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)
 1148:  -v_c\frac1m\ell_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)
 1149:  \xrightarrow{\Pr}0.
 1150: \]
 1151: Together with \eqref{eq:critical-uniform-layer}, this proves
 1152: \eqref{eq:critical-uniform-coexistence}.
 1153: \end{proof}
 1154: 
 1155: \begin{proposition}[Finite-prime-support coefficient interface]
 1156: \label{prop:prime-support-interface}
 1157: Fix distinct primes \(\mathcal P=\{p_1,\ldots,p_d\}\).  For
 1158: \(\mathbf a=(a_1,\ldots,a_d)\in\mathbb N^d\setminus\{\mathbf0\}\), put
 1159: \[
 1160:  q_{\mathbf a}:=\prod_{i=1}^d p_i^{a_i},

 succeeded in 348ms:
 1150: \]
 1151: Together with \eqref{eq:critical-uniform-layer}, this proves
 1152: \eqref{eq:critical-uniform-coexistence}.
 1153: \end{proof}
 1154: 
 1155: \begin{proposition}[Finite-prime-support coefficient interface]
 1156: \label{prop:prime-support-interface}
 1157: Fix distinct primes \(\mathcal P=\{p_1,\ldots,p_d\}\).  For
 1158: \(\mathbf a=(a_1,\ldots,a_d)\in\mathbb N^d\setminus\{\mathbf0\}\), put
 1159: \[
 1160:  q_{\mathbf a}:=\prod_{i=1}^d p_i^{a_i},
 1161:  \qquad
 1162:  A_{\mathbf a}(u):=
 1163:  \sum_{\substack{1\le h<q_{\mathbf a}\\(h,q_{\mathbf a})=1}}
 1164:  u^{c(h/q_{\mathbf a})},
 1165: \]
 1166: and define the formal series
 1167: \[
 1168:  A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,u)
 1169:  :=\sum_{\mathbf a\ne\mathbf0}
 1170:  A_{\mathbf a}(u)\mathbf z^{\mathbf a}.
 1171: \]
 1172: If
 1173: \[
 1174:  U_j(q_{\mathbf n})
 1175:  :=\card{\{g:\text{\(g\) is a Weinstein generator, }
 1176:  R(g)=q_{\mathbf n},\ L(g)=j\}},
 1177: \]
 1178: with the empty-word convention \(U_0(1)=1\), then
 1179: \begin{equation}
 1180:  \sum_{\mathbf n\in\mathbb N^d}\sum_{j\ge0}
 1181:  U_j(q_{\mathbf n})\mathbf z^{\mathbf n}u^j
 1182:  =\frac1{1-A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,u)}.
 1183:  \label{eq:prime-support-joint-renewal}
 1184: \end{equation}
 1185: For \(q_{\mathbf n}>2\), the exact finite-window count is
 1186: \begin{equation}
 1187:  C_m(q_{\mathbf n})
 1188:  =\sum_{j\ge0}K_m(j)U_j(q_{\mathbf n}).
 1189:  \label{eq:prime-support-window-interface}
 1190: \end{equation}
 1191: After the cost mark is removed, the series is rational:
 1192: \begin{equation}
 1193:  \sum_{\mathbf n\in\mathbb N^d}
 1194:  \left(\sum_{j\ge0}U_j(q_{\mathbf n})\right)\mathbf z^{\mathbf n}
 1195:  =\frac1{\displaystyle
 1196:  2-\prod_{i=1}^d\frac{1-z_i}{1-p_i z_i}}.
 1197:  \label{eq:prime-support-unmarked-rational}
 1198: \end{equation}
 1199: \end{proposition}
 1200: 
 1201: \begin{proof}
 1202: Under Weinstein's free-monoid parameterization, a letter with denominator
 1203: \(q_{\mathbf a}\) contributes exponent vector \(\mathbf a\), cost
 1204: \(c(h/q_{\mathbf a})\), and multiplier \(q_{\mathbf a}\).  Unique ordered
 1205: factorization into letters gives \eqref{eq:prime-support-joint-renewal} by the
 1206: formal sequence construction.  The four orbit branches counted in
 1207: \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count} depend only on the generator cost and
 1208: give \eqref{eq:prime-support-window-interface}.  Finally,
 1209: \[
 1210:  \sum_{a\ge0}\phi_{\rm E}(p_i^a)z_i^a
 1211:  =\frac{1-z_i}{1-p_i z_i}.
 1212: \]
 1213: Multiplying over the primes shows that
 1214: \(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)\) is the displayed product minus one, which
 1215: proves \eqref{eq:prime-support-unmarked-rational}.
 1216: \end{proof}
 1217: 
 1218: % The former directional quenched theorem is withdrawn: its proof required a
 1219: % parameter-uniform smooth-point expansion and an unbounded-cost truncation
 1220: % estimate that are not established here.
 1221: \iffalse
 1222: Write
 1223: \[
 1224:  \mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}:=
 1225:  \{\boldsymbol\rho\in(0,\infty)^d:
 1226:    \rho_1+\cdots+\rho_d=1\}.
 1227: \]
 1228: 
 1229: \begin{theorem}[Directional quenched law on finite prime support]
 1230: \label{thm:prime-support-directional-quenched}
 1231: Retain the notation of \Cref{prop:prime-support-interface}, and fix
 1232: \(\boldsymbol\rho\in\mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}\).  Put
 1233: \[
 1234:  g_i(z):=\frac{1-z}{1-p_i z},
 1235:  \qquad
 1236:  h_i(z):=\frac{z g_i'(z)}{g_i(z)}
 1237:  =\frac{(p_i-1)z}{(1-z)(1-p_i z)}.
 1238: \]
 1239: There are unique \(\tau>0\) and
 1240: \(\mathbf z=\mathbf z(\boldsymbol\rho)\in
 1241: \prod_i(0,p_i^{-1})\) such that
 1242: \begin{equation}
 1243:  \prod_{i=1}^d g_i(z_i)=2,
 1244:  \qquad h_i(z_i)=\tau\rho_i\quad(1\le i\le d).
 1245:  \label{eq:prime-support-directional-saddle}
 1246: \end{equation}
 1247: More explicitly, \(z_i=\zeta_i(\tau\rho_i)\), where
 1248: \begin{equation}
 1249:  \zeta_i(x)=
 1250:  \frac{(p_i-1)+(p_i+1)x-
 1251:   \sqrt{((p_i-1)+(p_i+1)x)^2-4p_i x^2}}
 1252:  {2p_i x}.
 1253:  \label{eq:prime-support-correct-inverse}
 1254: \end{equation}
 1255: The point \(\mathbf z\) is the unique point of
 1256: \(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf w,1)=1\) in the closed polydisc
 1257: \(\{|w_i|\le z_i\}\).
 1258: 
 1259: Give every letter \((\mathbf a,h/q_{\mathbf a})\) the probability
 1260: \begin{equation}
 1261:  \Pr_{\mathbf z}\{(\mathbf A,C)=
 1262:   (\mathbf a,c(h/q_{\mathbf a}))\}=\mathbf z^{\mathbf a}.
 1263:  \label{eq:prime-support-directional-letter-law}
 1264: \end{equation}
 1265: This is a probability law, and
 1266: \[
 1267:  \mathbb E_{\mathbf z}\mathbf A=2\tau\boldsymbol\rho,
 1268:  \qquad m_C(\boldsymbol\rho):=\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}C<\infty.
 1269: \]
 1270: Define its directional velocity by
 1271: \begin{equation}
 1272:  v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}:=
 1273:  \frac{2\tau\sum_i\rho_i\log p_i}{m_C(\boldsymbol\rho)}.
 1274:  \label{eq:prime-support-directional-velocity}
 1275: \end{equation}
 1276: 
 1277: Let \(\mathbf n_N=N\boldsymbol\rho+O(1)\in\mathbb N^d\), and let
 1278: \(W_N\) be uniform among the nonempty free generator words of total exponent
 1279: \(\mathbf n_N\).  If \(R_N\) is its number of letters and \(T_N\) its
 1280: total cost, then
 1281: \begin{equation}
 1282:  \frac{R_N}{N}\xrightarrow{\Pr}\frac1{2\tau},
 1283:  \qquad
 1284:  \frac{T_N}{N}\xrightarrow{\Pr}\frac{m_C(\boldsymbol\rho)}{2\tau},
 1285:  \qquad
 1286:  \frac{\log q_{\mathbf n_N}}{T_N}
 1287:  \xrightarrow{\Pr}v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}.
 1288:  \label{eq:prime-support-directional-free-law}
 1289: \end{equation}
 1290: 
 1291: Finally, let \(m_N\to\infty\) be integers such that
 1292: \begin{equation}
 1293:  \frac{\log q_{\mathbf n_N}}{m_N}\longrightarrow
 1294:  \alpha\in[0,v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}).
 1295:  \label{eq:prime-support-inactive-budget}
 1296: \end{equation}
 1297: Then the exact level
 1298: \(\{x\in X_{m_N}:d_{m_N}(x)=q_{\mathbf n_N}\}\) is nonempty for all
 1299: sufficiently large \(N\),
 1300: \begin{equation}
 1301:  C_{m_N}(q_{\mathbf n_N})
 1302:  \sim4\card{\{\text{free words of exponent }\mathbf n_N\}},
 1303:  \label{eq:prime-support-inactive-count}
 1304: \end{equation}
 1305: and, under the uniform law on this level,
 1306: \begin{equation}
 1307:  \frac{L(g_{m_N})}{m_N}
 1308:  \xrightarrow{\Pr}
 1309:  \frac{\alpha}{v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}}.
 1310:  \label{eq:prime-support-inactive-window-law}
 1311: \end{equation}
 1312: The free-word convergences are uniform when \(\boldsymbol\rho\) ranges over a
 1313: compact subset of \(\mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}\) and
 1314: \(\|\mathbf n_N-N\boldsymbol\rho\|\) is bounded.  The finite-window
 1315: conclusions are uniform under the additional requirement that the budget
 1316: ratio remain bounded away from one.
 1317: \end{theorem}
 1318: 
 1319: \begin{proof}
 1320: Each \(h_i\) is strictly increasing from zero to infinity on
 1321: \((0,p_i^{-1})\).  Solving the resulting quadratic gives
 1322: \eqref{eq:prime-support-correct-inverse}.  Hence
 1323: \(\prod_i g_i(\zeta_i(\tau\rho_i))\) increases continuously from one to
 1324: infinity, which proves existence and uniqueness in
 1325: \eqref{eq:prime-support-directional-saddle}.  Since
 1326: \(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)=1\),
 1327: \eqref{eq:prime-support-directional-letter-law} has total mass one, and
 1328: Euler differentiation gives
 1329: \[
 1330:  \mathbb E_{\mathbf z}A_i
 1331:  =z_i\partial_{z_i}A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)
 1332:  =2h_i(z_i)=2\tau\rho_i.
 1333: \]
 1334: 
 1335: We verify the strict-minimality and lattice hypotheses needed below.  If
 1336: \(|w_i|\le z_i\) and \(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf w,1)=1\), coefficient
 1337: positivity gives
 1338: \[
 1339:  1\le A_{\mathcal P}(|\mathbf w|,1)
 1340:  \le A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)=1.
 1341: \]
 1342: The support contains every \(\mathbf e_i\), so equality in the second
 1343: inequality forces \(|w_i|=z_i\) for every \(i\).  Equality in the triangle
 1344: inequality then forces all supported monomials to have phase one; applying
 1345: this to the \(\mathbf e_i\) gives \(w_i=z_i\).  The same support contains
 1346: \(2\mathbf e_i\), so its difference lattice is \(\mathbb Z^d\).  Finally,
 1347: the covariance matrix of \(\mathbf A\) is positive definite: if
 1348: \(\mathbf t\cdot\mathbf A\) were constant almost surely, comparison of
 1349: \(\mathbf e_i\) and \(2\mathbf e_i\) would give \(t_i=0\) for every
 1350: \(i\).
 1351: 
 1352: The fixed-denominator mean estimate quoted in the proof of
 1353: \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} gives
 1354: \[
 1355:  \sum_{\substack{1\le h<q\\(h,q)=1}}c(h/q)
 1356:  =O(q(1+\log q)^2).
 1357: \]
 1358: Since \(p_i z_i<1\), summation over exponent vectors proves
 1359: \(m_C(\boldsymbol\rho)<\infty\).
 1360: 
 1361: We now apply the smooth strictly minimal point expansion of
 1362: Pemantle--Wilson \cite{PemantleWilson2002}; the preceding paragraph verifies
 1363: its nonperiodicity, strict minimality, and nondegenerate transverse Hessian
 1364: for the present rational function.  Marking each letter by \(e^s\) and
 1365: using the expansion uniformly for small complex \(s\) gives the usual
 1366: analytic quasi-powers estimate.  The critical number of letters is determined
 1367: by
 1368: \[
 1369:  r_N\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}\mathbf A=N\boldsymbol\rho,
 1370:  \qquad\text{hence}\qquad r_N=\frac{N}{2\tau}.
 1371: \]
 1372: It follows that \(R_N/N\to1/(2\tau)\) in probability.
 1373: 
 1374: For \(M>0\), mark instead the bounded cost \(C\wedge M\).  The same analytic
 1375: perturbation gives
 1376: \begin{equation}
 1377:  \frac1N\sum_{j=1}^{R_N}(C_j\wedge M)
 1378:  \xrightarrow{\Pr}\frac{\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}(C\wedge M)}{2\tau}.
 1379:  \label{eq:prime-support-truncated-cost-law}
 1380: \end{equation}
 1381: To remove the truncation, put
 1382: \[
 1383:  D_M(\mathbf x):=
 1384:  \sum_{\text{letters }\ell}C(\ell)\mathbf1_{\{C(\ell)>M\}}
 1385:  \mathbf x^{\mathbf A(\ell)}.
 1386: \]
 1387: This series is analytic in a neighborhood of \(\mathbf z\).  Pointing one
 1388: letter in a free word gives the exact coefficient identity
 1389: \[
 1390:  \mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^{R_N}C_j\mathbf1_{\{C_j>M\}}
 1391:  =\frac{[\mathbf x^{\mathbf n_N}]
 1392:   D_M(\mathbf x)(1-A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf x,1))^{-2}}
 1393:  {[\mathbf x^{\mathbf n_N}]
 1394:   (1-A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf x,1))^{-1}}.
 1395: \]
 1396: The corresponding smooth-point expansions, or equivalently differentiation
 1397: of the analytic perturbation
 1398: \(A_{\mathcal P}+sD_M\) at \(s=0\), yield
 1399: \[
 1400:  \frac1N\mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^{R_N}
 1401:  C_j\mathbf1_{\{C_j>M\}}
 1402:  \longrightarrow
 1403:  \frac{\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}[C\mathbf1_{\{C>M\}}]}{2\tau}.
 1404: \]
 1405: First let \(N\to\infty\), then \(M\to\infty\), and use Markov's
 1406: inequality together with \eqref{eq:prime-support-truncated-cost-law}.  This
 1407: proves the cost limit in \eqref{eq:prime-support-directional-free-law}; the
 1408: velocity limit follows from
 1409: \(N^{-1}\log q_{\mathbf n_N}\to\sum_i\rho_i\log p_i\).
 1410: 
 1411: Under \eqref{eq:prime-support-inactive-budget}, the free law gives
 1412: \[
 1413:  \frac{T_N}{m_N}\xrightarrow{\Pr}
 1414:  \frac{\alpha}{v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}}<1.
 1415: \]
 1416: Thus \(\Pr\{T_N\le m_N-1\}\to1\).  By
 1417: \eqref{eq:prime-support-window-interface}, the finite-window law on generator
 1418: words is obtained from the uniform free-word law by the weight
 1419: \(K_{m_N}(T_N)\).  Since this weight is bounded by four and equals four on
 1420: an event of probability tending to one, its normalizing constant tends to
 1421: four and the two laws converge in total variation.  This proves
 1422: \eqref{eq:prime-support-inactive-count} and
 1423: \eqref{eq:prime-support-inactive-window-law}.
 1424: 
 1425: On compact subsets of \(\mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}\), the saddle remains in a
 1426: compact subset of \(\prod_i(0,p_i^{-1})\), the transverse covariance stays
 1427: uniformly nondegenerate, and the fixed-denominator bound supplies a common
 1428: summable majorant for the cost tails.  The smooth-point expansions and all
 1429: truncation estimates above are therefore uniform there.
 1430: \end{proof}
 1431: \fi
 1432: 
 1433: \begin{remark}[Located active-cutoff obstruction]
 1434: \label{rem:prime-support-local-open-interface}
 1435: Equations \eqref{eq:prime-support-joint-renewal} and
 1436: \eqref{eq:prime-support-window-interface} locate, but do not solve, the
 1437: active arithmetic interface.  The unmarked rational function is in the scope
 1438: of smooth-point multivariate coefficient methods such as
 1439: Pemantle--Wilson \cite{PemantleWilson2002}; the nearest ordered-factorization
 1440: comparators are Lau \cite{Lau2001} and Hwang--Janson
 1441: \cite{HwangJanson2011}.  Those results do not retain the continued-fraction
 1442: cost at an exponent-conditioned lattice point with an error uniform in the
 1443: direction, and they do not provide the uniform integrability needed for the
 1444: unbounded cost.  Accordingly, no directional quenched law is asserted here,
 1445: even below the active cutoff.
 1446: 
 1447: Already for \(\mathcal P=\{2\}\), the normalized letter law used in the
 1448: dyadic renewal assigns mass \(3^{-a}\) to each fraction with denominator
 1449: \(2^a\).  Since
 1450: \(c(1/2^a)=2^{a+1}-1\), its cost \(C\) satisfies
 1451: \[
 1452:  \mathbb E C^2
 1453:  \ge\sum_{a\ge1}3^{-a}(2^{a+1}-1)^2=+\infty,
 1454:  \qquad
 1455:  \mathbb E e^{\theta C}=+\infty\quad(\theta>0).
 1456: \]
 1457: Its first moment is finite: the Panov--Liehl mean estimate used above makes
 1458: the contribution at exponent \(a\) at most
 1459: \(O(a^2(2/3)^a)\).  Thus neither a Gaussian local theorem nor a two-sided
 1460: Cram\'er tilt is available at \(u=1\).  A sharp asymptotic for
 1461: \(C_m(q_{\mathbf n})\) with the cutoff \(j=m+O(1)\) requires a lattice
 1462: stable or semistable local-renewal theorem uniform under multivariate exponent
 1463: conditioning, followed by the nonsmooth weights \(K_m(m)=3\) and
 1464: \(K_m(m+1)=1\).  No such theorem is proved or invoked here; this is the exact
 1465: remaining open input.
 1466: \end{remark}
 1467: 
 1468: \fi
 1469: \ifdefined\supplementonly
 1470: \begin{theorem}[Finite-window conditional phase separation]
 1471: \label{thm:conditional-phase-separation}
 1472: Fix \(0<\alpha<v_c\) and
 1473: \[
 1474:  0<\varepsilon<\min\{\alpha,v_c-\alpha\}.
 1475: \]
 1476: Let \(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}\) be uniform on
 1477: \[
 1478:  \left\{x\in X_m:
 1479:  \left|m^{-1}\log d_m(x)-\alpha\right|<\varepsilon\right\}.
 1480: \]

 succeeded in 348ms:
    1: \section{Thermodynamics of the Fibonacci partition function}
    2: \label{sec:standard-thermodynamics}
    3: 
    4: The renewal identity was derived from Weinstein's standard Fibonacci
    5: partition function before it was transferred to the finite-window fold.  We
    6: now state its one-layer consequences intrinsically.  This removes the bespoke
    7: fold from the probabilistic conclusions.
    8: 
    9: For \(m\ge1\), recall
   10: \[
   11:  \mathcal I_m=[F_{m+1}-1,F_{m+2}-1)
   12: \]
   13: and define
   14: \[
   15:  Z_m^R(t):=\sum_{n\in\mathcal I_m}R(n)^t
   16:  \qquad(t\in\RR).
   17: \]
   18: If \(\mathbf N_m\) is uniform on \(\mathcal I_m\), put
   19: \[
   20:  Y_m^R:=\frac1m\log R(\mathbf N_m).
   21: \]
   22: 
   23: \begin{theorem}[One-layer Fibonacci partition thermodynamics]
   24: \label{thm:standard-fibonacci-thermodynamics}
   25: For every \(t\in\RR\),
   26: \begin{equation}
   27:  \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log Z_m^R(t)=P(t),
   28:  \label{eq:standard-layer-pressure}
   29: \end{equation}
   30: where \(P\) is the pressure in \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure}.  Thus the
   31: one-layer pressure is zero on \((-\infty,-\sigma_0]\), is positive, real
   32: analytic, and strictly convex on \((-\sigma_0,\infty)\), and has the
   33: one-sided derivatives in \eqref{eq:critical-pressure-slopes}.
   34: 
   35: For \(s>\sigma_0\), the unnormalized sums satisfy
   36: \begin{equation}
   37:  Z_m^R(-s)\longrightarrow
   38:  \mathcal Z_R(s):=
   39:  \frac2{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}-1.
   40:  \label{eq:standard-layer-frozen-limit}
   41: \end{equation}
   42: At the critical tilt,
   43: \begin{equation}
   44:  Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)\sim\frac{2m}{\mu_C}.
   45:  \label{eq:standard-layer-critical-size}
   46: \end{equation}
   47: 
   48: For \(n\in\mathcal I_m\) with \(R(n)>2\), let \(g(n)\) be the generating
   49: number of its Weinstein orbit and put \(\ell_m^R(n)=L(g(n))\); set
   50: \(\ell_m^R(n)=0\) when \(R(n)\le2\).  Let \(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m\) have the
   51: critical Gibbs law
   52: \[
   53:  \Pr\{\widehat{\mathbf N}_m=n\}
   54:  =\frac{R(n)^{-\sigma_0}}{Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)}
   55:  \qquad(n\in\mathcal I_m).
   56: \]
   57: Then
   58: \begin{equation}
   59:  \left(
   60:   \frac1m\log R(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m),
   61:   \frac1m\ell_m^R(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m)
   62:  \right)
   63:  \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\mathrm d}(v_cU,U),
   64:  \label{eq:standard-layer-critical-gibbs}
   65: \end{equation}
   66: where \(U\) is uniform on \([0,1]\).
   67: 
   68: Finally, \((Y_m^R)\) satisfies a full large deviation principle with speed
   69: \(m\) and the good rate function
   70: \begin{equation}
   71:  I(\alpha)=\sup_{t\in\RR}
   72:  \{t\alpha-P(t)+\log\varphi\}.
   73:  \label{eq:standard-layer-ldp-rate}
   74: \end{equation}
   75: Its effective domain is \([0,\alpha_+]\), where
   76: \(\alpha_+=\tfrac12\log\varphi\), and
   77: \[
   78:  I(\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha
   79:  \qquad(0\le\alpha\le v_c).
   80: \]
   81: \end{theorem}
   82: 
   83: \begin{proof}
   84: Put \(H_m(s)=\sum_{j=0}^m u_j(s)\).  The exact one-layer renewal identity
   85: \eqref{eq:weighted-layer-renewal} says
   86: \begin{equation}
   87:  Z_m^R(-s)=2H_{m-1}(s)+u_m(s)-1.
   88:  \label{eq:standard-layer-renewal}
   89: \end{equation}
   90: The proof of \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} established
   91: \[
   92:  \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log u_m(s)
   93:  =\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log H_m(s)
   94:  =P(-s).
   95: \]
   96: Since \(u_m(s)\le Z_m^R(-s)\le3H_m(s)\), this proves
   97: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-pressure}.
   98: 
   99: If \(s>\sigma_0\), then \(U_s(1)=\sum_{j\ge0}u_j(s)<\infty\), so
  100: \(u_m(s)\to0\).  Equations
  101: \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function} and
  102: \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one} give
  103: \[
  104:  U_s(1)=\frac1{1-B_s(1)}
  105:  =\frac1{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}.
  106: \]
  107: Letting \(m\to\infty\) in \eqref{eq:standard-layer-renewal} proves
  108: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-frozen-limit}.  At \(s=\sigma_0\), the arithmetic
  109: renewal theorem used in \Cref{thm:critical-finite-window-gibbs} gives
  110: \(u_m(\sigma_0)\to1/\mu_C\).  Ces\`aro summation in
  111: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-renewal} proves
  112: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-size}.
  113: 
  114: We next retain the generating cost.  The four orbit branches displayed above
  115: \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count} show that a generator of cost \(c\)
  116: contributes two points to \(\mathcal I_m\) if \(c<m\), one if \(c=m\), and
  117: none if \(c>m\).  Thus the one-layer boundary weight is
  118: \[
  119:  K_m^R(c)=
  120:  \begin{cases}
  121:   2,&c<m,\\
  122:   1,&c=m,\\
  123:   0,&c>m.
  124:  \end{cases}
  125: \]
  126: With the marked renewal measure \(\mathcal U\) defined before
  127: \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count}, the same free-monoid bijection gives,
  128: for every Borel set
  129: \(B\subset\mathbb R\times(\log2,\infty)\),
  130: \begin{equation}
  131:  \card{\{n\in\mathcal I_m:
  132:    (\ell_m^R(n),\log R(n))\in B\}}
  133:  =\int_B K_m^R(c)e^{\sigma_0h}\,\mathcal U(dc,dh).
  134:  \label{eq:standard-layer-marked-count}
  135: \end{equation}
  136: The points with \(R(n)\le2\) have bounded total critical weight, uniformly in
  137: \(m\), by the uniform layer-count lemma.
  138: 
  139: For every bounded continuous \(f\) on \([0,1]\), the arithmetic renewal
  140: theorem and a Riemann-sum argument give
  141: \[
  142:  \frac1m\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(\sigma_0)f(j/m)
  143:  \longrightarrow\frac1{\mu_C}\int_0^1f(y)\,dy.
  144: \]
  145: After multiplication of \eqref{eq:standard-layer-marked-count} by
  146: \(e^{-\sigma_0h}\), the weights are two for \(j<m\) and one at \(j=m\).
  147: Together with \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-size}, this proves
  148: \[
  149:  \frac{\ell_m^R(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m)}m
  150:  \xrightarrow{\mathrm d}U.
  151: \]
  152: For the reward coordinate, the critical renewal increments
  153: \((C_i,H_i)\) satisfy
  154: \[
  155:  \frac{H_1+\cdots+H_r}{C_1+\cdots+C_r}
  156:  \longrightarrow\frac{\mu_H}{\mu_C}=v_c
  157: \quad\text{almost surely}.
  158: \]
  159: Every cost increment is at least three.  Consequently the number of renewal
  160: epochs with total cost at most \(m\) and reward-to-cost ratio outside a fixed
  161: \(\delta\)-neighborhood of \(v_c\) is \(o(m)\), pathwise and, by bounded
  162: convergence after division by \(m\), in expectation.  Since \(K_m^R\le2\),
  163: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-marked-count} and
  164: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-size} imply
  165: \[
  166:  \frac1m\log R(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m)
  167:  -v_c\frac{\ell_m^R(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m)}m
  168:  \xrightarrow{\Pr}0.
  169: \]
  170: This proves \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-gibbs}.
  171: 
  172: It remains to prove the full LDP, in particular the lower bound at the
  173: nonexposed slopes.  The layer has
  174: \(\card{\mathcal I_m}=F_m\), and hence
  175: \[
  176:  \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log\card{\mathcal I_m}=\log\varphi.
  177: \]
  178: Equation \eqref{eq:standard-layer-pressure} therefore gives the limiting
  179: logarithmic moment generating function
  180: \[
  181:  \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m
  182:  \log\mathbb E e^{tmY_m^R}=P(t)-\log\varphi.
  183:  \label{eq:standard-layer-log-mgf}
  184: \]
  185: The translated extremal theorem in the Fibonacci-convention lemma gives
  186: \[
  187:  \max_{n\in\mathcal I_m}\frac1m\log R(n)
  188:  \longrightarrow\alpha_+.
  189:  \label{eq:standard-layer-maximum}
  190: \]
  191: Indeed, the two shifted endpoints have partition value one, so they do not
  192: change the maximum on the intervening Fibonacci block.  Thus the variables
  193: are exponentially tight, and the G\"artner--Ellis upper bound gives the
  194: required upper bound for closed sets.
  195: 
  196: For the lower bound, first let \(t>t_c=-\sigma_0\), write
  197: \(v=P'(t)>v_c\), and
  198: tilt the uniform law on \(\mathcal I_n\) by \(R(k)^t\).  Analyticity of \(P\)
  199: and the exponential Markov inequality applied to
  200: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-pressure} show that this tilted law assigns
  201: exponentially vanishing mass to
  202: \[
  203:  \left\{k\in\mathcal I_n:
  204:    \left|n^{-1}\log R(k)-v\right|\ge\delta\right\}.
  205: \]
  206: Consequently, if
  207: \[
  208:  A_n^R(v,\delta)
  209:  :=\card{\{k\in\mathcal I_n:
  210:  |n^{-1}\log R(k)-v|<\delta\}},
  211: \]
  212: then
  213: \begin{equation}
  214:  \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log A_n^R(v,\delta)
  215:  \ge P(t)-tv-|t|\delta.
  216:  \label{eq:standard-layer-exposed-source}
  217: \end{equation}
  218: 
  219: For large \(n\), every point counted here has multiplicity greater than two.
  220: Each generating orbit contributes at most two points to \(\mathcal I_n\).
  221: Keeping one generator from each represented orbit therefore produces a set
  222: \(\mathcal G_n^R\) with
  223: \[
  224:  \card{\mathcal G_n^R}\ge\tfrac12A_n^R(v,\delta),
  225:  \qquad L(g)\le n\quad(g\in\mathcal G_n^R),
  226: \]
  227: and \(n^{-1}\log R(g)\in(v-\delta,v+\delta)\).
  228: 
  229: Fix \(0<\alpha<\alpha_+\).  Choose \(t>t_c\) so that
  230: \(v=P'(t)>\alpha\): any \(t>t_c\) works when \(\alpha\le v_c\), while for
  231: \(\alpha>v_c\) take \(t>t_\alpha\), where
  232: \(P'(t_\alpha)=\alpha\).  Only after this choice set
  233: \(\rho=\alpha/v<1\), and put
  234: \(n_m=\lfloor\rho m\rfloor\).  For \(g\in\mathcal G_{n_m}^R\), define
  235: \[
  236:  z_m(g)=
  237:  \begin{cases}
  238:   [a]g,&m-L(g)=2a,\\
  239:   [a]\tau g,&m-L(g)=2a+1.
  240:  \end{cases}
  241: \]
  242: The orbit formulas give \(L(z_m(g))=m\) and
  243: \(R(z_m(g))=R(g)\), so \(z_m(g)\in\mathcal I_m\).  Freeness makes this map
  244: injective.  From \eqref{eq:standard-layer-exposed-source}, first letting
  245: \(\delta\downarrow0\), we obtain the local counting lower bound
  246: \begin{equation}
  247:  \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\liminf_{m\to\infty}\frac1m
  248:  \log A_m^R(\alpha,\varepsilon)
  249:  \ge\frac{\alpha}{v}\bigl(P(t)-tv\bigr).
  250:  \label{eq:standard-layer-orbit-filling}
  251: \end{equation}
  252: 
  253: If \(0<\alpha\le v_c\), let \(t\downarrow t_c\) through the choices above in
  254: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-orbit-filling}; then \(v\downarrow v_c\) and the
  255: right side tends to \(\sigma_0\alpha\).  If
  256: \(v_c<\alpha<\alpha_+\), use the \(t_\alpha\) just defined and let
  257: \(t\downarrow t_\alpha\) through values with
  258: \(P'(t)>\alpha\).  The right side then tends to
  259: \(P(t_\alpha)-t_\alpha\alpha\).  In both cases this is
  260: \(\log\varphi-I(\alpha)\).  Division by
  261: \(\card{\mathcal I_m}=F_m\) proves the LDP lower bound at every interior
  262: point of the effective domain.
  263: 
  264: The effective domain and the affine formula follow from the same convex-dual
  265: calculation as in \Cref{thm:full-fiber-ldp}, since the pressure is the same.
  266: At \(0\), the left endpoint \(F_{m+1}-1\) has \(R=1\) and supplies the lower
  267: bound directly.  At \(\alpha_+\), use interior points tending to the endpoint
  268: and continuity of the finite convex rate on \([0,\alpha_+]\).  Hence the
  269: lower bound holds for every open set, completing the full LDP.
  270: \end{proof}
  271: 
  272: For the same uniform point \(\mathbf N_m\), retain the generating cost by
  273: putting
  274: \[
  275:  B_m^R:=\frac1m\ell_m^R(\mathbf N_m).
  276: \]
  277: 
  278: \begin{theorem}[Joint generation-cost and multiplicity large deviations]
  279: \label{thm:joint-cost-reward-ldp}
  280: The vectors \((B_m^R,Y_m^R)\) satisfy a full large deviation principle on
  281: \(\mathbb R^2\) with speed \(m\).  Their limiting logarithmic moment generating
  282: function is
  283: \begin{equation}
  284:  \Lambda(a,t)=\max\{0,a+P(t)\}-\log\varphi
  285:  \qquad(a,t\in\mathbb R).
  286:  \label{eq:joint-cost-reward-log-mgf}
  287: \end{equation}
  288: The good rate function is
  289: \begin{equation}
  290:  J(\beta,\alpha)=
  291:  \begin{cases}
  292:   (1-\beta)\log\varphi+\beta I(\alpha/\beta),
  293:    &0<\beta\le1,\quad 0\le\alpha\le\beta\alpha_+,\\
  294:   \log\varphi,&(\beta,\alpha)=(0,0),\\
  295:   +\infty,&\text{otherwise}.
  296:  \end{cases}
  297:  \label{eq:joint-cost-reward-rate}
  298: \end{equation}
  299: In particular, the rate surface has the affine face
  300: \begin{equation}
  301:  J(\beta,\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha
  302:  \qquad(0\le\beta\le1,\ 0\le\alpha\le\beta v_c).
  303:  \label{eq:joint-cost-reward-flat-face}
  304: \end{equation}
  305: \end{theorem}
  306: 
  307: \begin{proof}
  308: Let \(\mathcal W_j\) be the set of nonempty free generator words of total
  309: cost \(j\), and write \(Q(w)\) for the product of the denominators of the
  310: letters of \(w\).  By \eqref{eq:generator-weight-length},
  311: \begin{equation}
  312:  u_j(-t)=\sum_{w\in\mathcal W_j}Q(w)^t.
  313:  \label{eq:fixed-cost-word-partition}
  314: \end{equation}
  315: The convention \(\ell_m^R=0\) at multiplicities at most two changes only a
  316: bounded number of states.  The one-layer orbit weights in
  317: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-marked-count} therefore give, up to a term bounded
  318: for each fixed \((a,t)\),
  319: \begin{equation}
  320:  \sum_{n\in\mathcal I_m}e^{a\ell_m^R(n)}R(n)^t
  321:  =2\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}e^{aj}u_j(-t)+e^{am}u_m(-t)+O_{a,t}(1).
  322:  \label{eq:joint-cost-reward-renewal}
  323: \end{equation}
  324: More precisely, the bounded correction removes the level-two generator orbit
  325: from its positive cost and records it at cost zero; the level-one endpoint is
  326: already a cost-zero term.
  327: 
  328: The proof of \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} established
  329: \(\lim_{j\to\infty}j^{-1}\log u_j(-t)=P(t)\), and \(u_j(-t)>0\) for every
  330: \(j\ge8\).  Applying these facts to the partial sum in
  331: \eqref{eq:joint-cost-reward-renewal}, while retaining the level-one endpoint,
  332: gives
  333: \[
  334:  \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log
  335:  \sum_{n\in\mathcal I_m}e^{a\ell_m^R(n)}R(n)^t
  336:  =\max\{0,a+P(t)\}.
  337: \]
  338: Since \(\card{\mathcal I_m}=F_m\), this proves
  339: \eqref{eq:joint-cost-reward-log-mgf}.
  340: 
  341: We next compute its convex dual.  For fixed \(t\),
  342: \[
  343:  \sup_{a\in\mathbb R}\{a\beta-\max(0,a+P(t))\}
  344:  =\begin{cases}
  345:    -\beta P(t),&0\le\beta\le1,\\
  346:    +\infty,&\text{otherwise}.
  347:   \end{cases}
  348: \]
  349: For \(0<\beta\le1\), subsequent optimization over \(t\) gives
  350: \[
  351:  \log\varphi+\beta\sup_t\{t\alpha/\beta-P(t)\}
  352:  =(1-\beta)\log\varphi+\beta I(\alpha/\beta).
  353: \]
  354: At \(\beta=0\), the dual is finite only at \(\alpha=0\), where it equals
  355: \(\log\varphi\).  The effective domain of \(I\) from
  356: \Cref{thm:standard-fibonacci-thermodynamics} now gives exactly
  357: \eqref{eq:joint-cost-reward-rate}.  The variables are supported in a common
  358: compact set up to a vanishing enlargement: \(0\le B_m^R\le1\), and
  359: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-maximum} bounds \(Y_m^R\).  Thus the
  360: G\"artner--Ellis upper bound applies to every closed set.
  361: 
  362: It remains to prove the lower bound, because the interior points with
  363: \(0<\beta<1\) are not exposed points of \(\Lambda\).  Fix \(t>t_c\) and put
  364: \(v=P'(t)>v_c\).  On \(\mathcal W_j\), use the probability proportional to
  365: \(Q(w)^t\).  For sufficiently small real \(s\),
  366: \eqref{eq:fixed-cost-word-partition} and the fixed-cost pressure limit give
  367: \[
  368:  \limsup_{j\to\infty}\frac1j\log
  369:  \Pr_t\left\{\left|j^{-1}\log Q(w)-v\right|\ge\delta\right\}<0.
  370: \]
  371: This is the same two-sided exponential Markov argument as in
  372: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-exposed-source}.  Consequently,
  373: \begin{equation}
  374:  \liminf_{j\to\infty}\frac1j\log
  375:  \card{\{w\in\mathcal W_j:\ |j^{-1}\log Q(w)-v|<\delta\}}
  376:  \ge P(t)-tv-|t|\delta.
  377:  \label{eq:fixed-cost-exposed-source}
  378: \end{equation}
  379: 
  380: Let \(0<\beta\le1\) and suppose first that
  381: \(\alpha/\beta=v>v_c\).  Taking integers \(j_m\) with \(j_m/m\to\beta\),
  382: each word counted in \eqref{eq:fixed-cost-exposed-source} determines at least
  383: one point of \(\mathcal I_m\), because a generator orbit of cost \(j_m\le m\)
  384: meets that layer.  Freeness preserves injectivity.  Hence the local counting
  385: exponent is at least
  386: \(\beta(P(t)-t\alpha/\beta)\), which, after division by \(F_m\), is
  387: \(-J(\beta,\alpha)\).  The same conclusion at \(v_c\) follows by approximation
  388: from \(t\downarrow t_c\).
  389: 
  390: Now let \(0<\alpha\le\beta v_c\).  Keep \(t>t_c\), put \(v=P'(t)\), and choose
  391: integers
  392: \[
  393:  k_m/m\longrightarrow\alpha/v,\qquad j_m/m\longrightarrow\beta.
  394: \]
  395: Then \(r_m:=j_m-k_m\) tends linearly to infinity.  Every integer \(r\ge6\)
  396: is the total cost of at most two letters with logarithmic multiplier
  397: \(O(\log r)\): if \(r\) is odd, use the letter \(1/q\) with
  398: \(q=(r+1)/2\), whose cost is \(2q-1=r\); if \(r\) is even, use \(1/2\) and
  399: \(1/q\) with \(q=(r-2)/2\).  Append this fixed suffix to every source word of
  400: cost \(k_m\) counted in \eqref{eq:fixed-cost-exposed-source}.  The free-monoid
  401: property again gives an injection, the total cost is exactly \(j_m\), and the
  402: added reward is \(o(m)\).  The resulting local counting exponent is at least
  403: \[
  404:  \frac{\alpha}{v}\{P(t)-tv\}.
  405: \]
  406: Letting \(t\downarrow t_c=-\sigma_0\), and using \(P(t)\to0\) and
  407: \(v\downarrow v_c\), gives the exponent \(\sigma_0\alpha\).  For \(\alpha=0\)
  408: the filler word alone gives exponent zero.  After normalization by \(F_m\),
  409: these bounds equal the negative of
  410: \eqref{eq:joint-cost-reward-flat-face}.  The point \((0,0)\) is supplied by
  411: the level-one endpoint.  Finally, points with
  412: \(\alpha=\beta\alpha_+\) follow by approximation from the relative interior;
  413: the finite convex rate in \eqref{eq:joint-cost-reward-rate} is continuous on
  414: its compact effective domain.  This proves the lower bound for every open set
  415: and completes the full LDP.
  416: \end{proof}
  417: 
  418: \begin{remark}[Open second-order critical-cost interface]
  419: \label{rem:critical-cost-tail-open-interface}
  420: The preceding theorem uses only exponential rates and does not identify a
  421: second-order critical renewal term.  Such a result would require, at a
  422: minimum, a uniform mixed-denominator estimate of the form
  423: \[
  424:  \sum_{q\ge2}q^{-\sigma_0}
  425:  \card{\{1\le p<q:(p,q)=1,\ c(p/q)>x\}}
  426:  \sim K_Cx^{1-\sigma_0}\qquad(x\to\infty)
  427: \]
  428: with \(K_C>0\), including a proof that multiple large partial quotients and
  429: possible lattice oscillations are lower order.  Fixed-denominator estimates
  430: such as \cite{AistleitnerBordaHauke2023} do not by themselves provide the
  431: uniform summation in the critical range \(q\asymp x\).  No such regular
  432: variation statement, and hence no \(m^{3-\sigma_0}\) correction to the
  433: critical partition sum, is asserted here.
  434: \end{remark}

 succeeded in 358ms:
  470: \(j\ge2k\), the \(N_1\)-branches contribute one point in layer \(j\), and
  471: their \(\sigma\)-images contribute one point because the corresponding
  472: \(N_1\)-branches meet layer \(j-1\).  The two sheets are disjoint, so each
  473: generating orbit contributes exactly two points.  Hence
  474: \(N_j(k)=2\Psi(k)\) for \(j\ge2k\).  Weinstein's Theorem~5.1 supplies the
  475: same conclusion away from the endpoint; the preceding argument proves the
  476: additional case \(j=2k\).
  477: 
  478: Finally, Weinstein often writes the auxiliary layer with both endpoints.
  479: The shared endpoint has partition value \(1\), so this distinction is
  480: irrelevant for \(k\ge2\); the level \(1\) endpoint was assigned directly
  481: above.  This completes the treatment of endpoints and early layers.
  482: \end{proof}
  483: 
  484: We now show that an all-real analyticity ansatz cannot hold.  The obstruction
  485: is a proved frozen half-line for the Fibonacci partition spectrum.  Let
  486: \(\sigma_0>2\) be the unique real solution of
  487: \begin{equation}
  488:  \frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}=2.
  489:  \label{eq:freezing-exponent}
  490: \end{equation}
  491: Numerically, \(\sigma_0=2.478750785733960\ldots\).
  492: The number \(-\sigma_0\) is initially obtained as a Dirichlet-series freezing
  493: bound.  The weighted renewal argument in
  494: \Cref{thm:exact-negative-pressure} subsequently proves that it is the exact
  495: zero-pressure boundary.
  496: 
  497: \begin{theorem}[Negative-temperature freezing]
  498: \label{thm:negative-temperature-freezing}
  499: For every real \(t\le-\sigma_0\), the pressure exists unconditionally and
  500: \[
  501:  P(t):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_t(m)=0.
  502: \]
  503: Consequently there is no real-analytic function on \(\RR\) which agrees with
  504: the finite-window pressure limits wherever those limits exist and also
  505: satisfies \(P(0)=\log\varphi\).  In particular, no everywhere finite pressure
  506: extension can be both real analytic and strictly convex on \(\RR\).
  507: \end{theorem}
  508: 
  509: \begin{proof}
  510: For \(s>2\), put
  511: \[
  512:  A(s):=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(n)}{n^s}
  513:  =\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}-1,
  514: \]
  515: where \(\phi_{\rm E}\) is Euler's totient function.  Weinstein's free-monoid
  516: decomposition and Tonelli's theorem give the following equality in
  517: \([0,+\infty]\):
  518: \begin{equation}
  519:  1+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^s}
  520:  =\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}A(s)^r.
  521:  \label{eq:weinstein-level-dirichlet-series}
  522: \end{equation}
  523: The geometric series is finite exactly when \(A(s)<1\), equivalently when
  524: \(\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)<2\); in that region its value is
  525: \((2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s))^{-1}\).  The totient Dirichlet series also shows
  526: that the ratio is strictly decreasing from \(+\infty\) to \(1\) on
  527: \((2,\infty)\).  Hence \Cref{eq:freezing-exponent} has exactly one solution,
  528: and \Cref{eq:weinstein-level-dirichlet-series} is finite precisely for
  529: \(s>\sigma_0\).
  530: 
  531: By \Cref{thm:fiber-partition-interval}, the collision sum is exactly the sum
  532: of \(R(n)^t\) over two adjacent Fibonacci layers:
  533: \begin{equation}
  534:  S_t(m)=\sum_{n=F_{m+1}-1}^{F_{m+3}-2}R(n)^t.
  535:  \label{eq:real-collision-two-layers}
  536: \end{equation}
  537: If \(t<-\sigma_0\), the uniform layer-count lemma and
  538: \Cref{eq:weinstein-level-dirichlet-series} give, uniformly in \(m\),
  539: \[
  540:  1\le S_t(m)
  541:  \le 2+4\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\Psi(k)k^t<\infty.
  542: \]
  543: Thus \(m^{-1}\log S_t(m)\to0\).
  544: 
  545: At \(t=-\sigma_0\), fix \(\varepsilon>0\).  Only levels \(k\le D_m\)
  546: occur, and for each such level
  547: \[
  548:  k^{-\sigma_0}\le D_m^\varepsilon k^{-(\sigma_0+\varepsilon)}.
  549: \]
  550: The same layer-count estimate therefore gives
  551: \[
  552:  S_{-\sigma_0}(m)
  553:  \le 2+4D_m^{\varepsilon}
  554:        \sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^{\sigma_0+\varepsilon}}.
  555: \]
  556: The series is finite, while
  557: \(m^{-1}\log D_m\to\frac12\log\varphi\) by
  558: \Cref{thm:unconditional-extremal-fibers}.  Therefore
  559: \[
  560:  0\le\limsup_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_{-\sigma_0}(m)
  561:  \le\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\log\varphi.
  562: \]
  563: Letting \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\) proves the endpoint assertion.
  564: 
  565: Finally, an analytic function which vanishes on the open interval
  566: \(( -\infty,-\sigma_0)\) vanishes identically, whereas
  567: \[
  568:  P(0)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log|X_m|=\log\varphi.
  569: \]
  570: This proves the analytic and strict-convexity obstructions.
  571: \end{proof}
  572: 
  573: \begin{proposition}[No globally analytic compact-operator representation]
  574: \label{prop:no-analytic-compact-representation}
  575: There do not exist a complex Banach space \(\mathcal B\), a connected open
  576: set \(U\subset\mathbb C\) containing \(\RR\), and an operator-norm analytic
  577: map
  578: \[
  579:  z\longmapsto\mathcal L_z\in\mathcal K(\mathcal B)
  580:  \qquad(z\in U)
  581: \]
  582: into the compact operators with the following properties.  For every real
  583: \(t\), the operator \(\mathcal L_t\) has a positive algebraically simple
  584: isolated eigenvalue \(r(t)\); for every \(t_0\in\RR\), this eigenvalue is
  585: separated from the remainder of the spectrum by a positive gap uniformly for
  586: \(t\) in some real neighborhood of \(t_0\); and the family represents the
  587: finite-window sums in the exponential sense
  588: \[
  589:  \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_t(m)=\log r(t)
  590:  \qquad(t\in\RR).
  591: \]
  592: \end{proposition}
  593: 
  594: \begin{proof}
  595: For each \(m\ge1\), the function
  596: \[
  597:  p_m(t):=\frac1m\log S_t(m)
  598: \]
  599: is convex on \(\RR\).  Indeed, all fiber multiplicities are positive by the
  600: interval-completeness lemma, and H\"older's inequality gives the defining
  601: convexity inequality for \(S_t(m)\).  The representation hypothesis says that
  602: \(p_m(t)\) converges pointwise on \(\RR\) to
  603: \[
  604:  P(t):=\log r(t).
  605: \]
  606: The finite pointwise limit \(P\) is therefore convex.  A finite convex function
  607: on the open interval \(\RR\) is continuous, so \(r(t)=e^{P(t)}\) is continuous
  608: on \(\RR\).
  609: 
  610: Fix \(t_0\in\RR\).  Choose a positively oriented contour \(\Gamma\) enclosing
  611: \(r(t_0)\) and no other point of the spectrum of \(\mathcal L_{t_0}\).  For
  612: complex \(z\) sufficiently close to \(t_0\), the contour remains in the
  613: resolvent set and the Riesz projection
  614: \[
  615:  \Pi(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_\Gamma
  616:   (\lambda I-\mathcal L_z)^{-1}\,d\lambda
  617: \]
  618: is analytic.  Its rank is locally constant and equals the algebraic
  619: multiplicity of \(r(t_0)\), hence is one.  Analytic perturbation theory
  620: \cite[Chap.~VII, Sec.~1]{Kato1995} therefore supplies a unique analytic
  621: eigenvalue branch \(\mu(z)\) inside \(\Gamma\), with
  622: \(\mu(t_0)=r(t_0)\).  The continuity of \(r(t)\) puts \(r(t)\) inside
  623: \(\Gamma\) for all real \(t\) sufficiently close to \(t_0\); the local
  624: spectral separation and rank-one Riesz projection then force
  625: \(r(t)=\mu(t)\).  Thus \(r\), and hence \(t\mapsto\log r(t)\), is locally real
  626: analytic at every \(t_0\).
  627: 
  628: The representation hypothesis makes this function equal to \(P(t)\).  By
  629: \Cref{thm:negative-temperature-freezing}, it vanishes on
  630: \(( -\infty,-\sigma_0)\), and therefore vanishes identically.  This
  631: contradicts \(P(0)=\log\varphi\).
  632: \end{proof}
  633: 
  634: The all-real renewal analysis in \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} shows that the
  635: pressure is real analytic and strictly convex on the positive-pressure phase,
  636: but it also shows that the present freezing transition has a genuine corner:
  637: the right derivative at \(-\sigma_0\) is strictly positive.  Thus the former
  638: differentiability hypothesis at the transition is false, and the
  639: G\"artner--Ellis theorem does not by itself provide lower bounds for slopes in
  640: the interval between the two one-sided derivatives.  The finite-window
  641: orbit-filling construction in \Cref{thm:full-fiber-ldp} provides precisely
  642: these missing lower bounds and yields an unconditional full large deviation
  643: principle.
    1: \section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
    2: 
    3: Let \(F_0=0\), \(F_1=F_2=1\), and
    4: \(F_{j+2}=F_{j+1}+F_j\), and put
    5: \(\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2\).  Let \(R(n)\) be the classical Fibonacci
    6: partition function: the number of partitions of \(n\) into distinct
    7: Fibonacci numbers, with the two unit terms identified.  Our main
    8: thermodynamic results concern \(R\) on the standard layers
    9: \[
   10:  \mathcal I_m=[F_{m+1}-1,F_{m+2}-1).
   11: \]
   12: The moment pressure for every real tilt and its first-order transition are
   13: already contained, under an affine normalization, in the golden-ratio
   14: Bernoulli-convolution literature \cite{LauNgai1998,FengOlivier2003,Feng2005}.
   15: We make that normalization explicit and use it as the starting point for
   16: finite-window identities, arithmetic critical refinements, and full
   17: large-deviation lower bounds for \(m^{-1}\log R(n)\) under the uniform law on
   18: \(\mathcal I_m\).
   19: 
   20: A finite-window Zeckendorf fold gives an exact finite model for this standard
   21: object.  A word
   22: \(\omega\in\{0,1\}^m\) determines the raw sum
   23: \[
   24:   N_m(\omega)=\sum_{j=1}^m\omega_jF_{j+1}.
   25: \]
   26: We consider the finite-window fold obtained by reducing this sum modulo
   27: \(F_{m+2}\) and taking the unique legal Zeckendorf word in the visible
   28: window.  If \(d_m(x)\) is the cardinality of the fiber over a legal word
   29: \(x\), the corresponding finite problem is to identify the multiset
   30: \(\{d_m(x):x\in X_m\}\) and its extremal and moment statistics.
   31: 
   32: The bridge is exact: the complete fiber list is the list of \(R\) on
   33: \(\mathcal I_m\sqcup\mathcal I_{m+1}\).  A second correspondence is an affine
   34: reindexing of a finite subset-sum spectrum.  Both are finite identities and
   35: do not require a normalization transducer.
   36: 
   37: \subsection{Main results}
   38: 
   39: Put \(R(n)=0\) for \(n<0\).  Let \(\sigma_0>2\) be the unique solution of
   40: \[
   41:  \frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}=2;
   42:  \qquad \sigma_0=2.4787507857\ldots.
   43: \]
   44: For \(t>-\sigma_0\), let \(P(t)>0\) be the unique solution of
   45: \[
   46:  \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
   47:  q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}=1,
   48: \]
   49: where \(c(p/q)\) is the odd continued-fraction cost defined before
   50: \Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal}, and put \(P(t)=0\) for
   51: \(t\le-\sigma_0\).  Set
   52: \[
   53:  \begin{gathered}
   54:  \mu_C:=\sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
   55:  c(p/q)q^{-\sigma_0},\\
   56:  v_c:=\frac{\displaystyle
   57:  \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
   58:  q^{-\sigma_0}\log q}{\mu_C},
   59:  \qquad
   60:  t_c:=-\sigma_0,\qquad
   61:  \alpha_+:=\frac12\log\varphi.
   62:  \end{gathered}
   63: \]
   64: Define
   65: \[
   66:  Z_m^R(t):=\sum_{n\in\mathcal I_m}R(n)^t.
   67: \]
   68: 
   69: \begin{theoremA}[One-layer Fibonacci partition thermodynamics]
   70: For every \(t\in\RR\),
   71: \[
   72:  \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log Z_m^R(t)=P(t).
   73: \]
   74: For \(s>\sigma_0\),
   75: \[
   76:  Z_m^R(-s)\longrightarrow
   77:  \frac2{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}-1,
   78: \]
   79: whereas \(Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)\sim2m/\mu_C\).  Under the critical Gibbs law
   80: proportional to \(R(n)^{-\sigma_0}\), the normalized log multiplicity and
   81: generating-layer cost converge jointly to \((v_cU,U)\), with \(U\) uniform on
   82: \([0,1]\).
   83: 
   84: If \(\mathbf N_m\) is uniform on \(\mathcal I_m\), then
   85: \(m^{-1}\log R(\mathbf N_m)\) satisfies a full large deviation principle
   86: with good rate function
   87: \[
   88:  I(\alpha)=\sup_{t\in\RR}\{t\alpha-P(t)+\log\varphi\}.
   89: \]
   90: Its effective domain is \([0,\alpha_+]\), and
   91: \(I(\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha\) on \([0,v_c]\).
   92: \end{theoremA}
   93: 
   94: This is \Cref{thm:standard-fibonacci-thermodynamics}.  Its proof uses the
   95: one-layer orbit weights \(2,1\), rather than deducing a statement about one
   96: component from the two-layer fiber mixture.
   97: 
   98: The same renewal representation retains the normalized generating cost
   99: \(B_m^R=m^{-1}\ell_m^R(\mathbf N_m)\).
  100: \Cref{thm:joint-cost-reward-ldp} proves a full LDP for
  101: \((B_m^R,Y_m^R)\).  Its rate is
  102: \[
  103:  J(\beta,\alpha)=
  104:  (1-\beta)\log\varphi+\beta I(\alpha/\beta)
  105:  \quad(0<\beta\le1,\ 0\le\alpha\le\beta\alpha_+),
  106: \]
  107: with \(J(0,0)=\log\varphi\) and \(J=+\infty\) elsewhere.  In particular,
  108: \[
  109:  J(\beta,\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha
  110:  \qquad(0\le\alpha\le\beta v_c),
  111: \]
  112: so coexistence is an affine face of the joint rate surface rather than an
  113: artifact of one-dimensional projection.  Fixed-cost exponential tilting
  114: supplies the strictly convex part; an exact one- or two-letter low-reward
  115: suffix fills every sufficiently large remaining cost on the affine face.
  116: 
  117: For the exact finite-window model, put
  118: \(R^\dagger(n)=R(n)+R(n-1)\).  Let
  119: \[
  120:   \widetilde d_m(n)=[z^n]\prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j}),
  121:   \qquad 0\le n<F_{m+2}.
  122: \]
  123: For residues modulo \(F_{m+2}\), define
  124: \[
  125:   \pi_m(n)\equiv F_{m+1}n+F_{m+1}-1\pmod{F_{m+2}}.
  126: \]
  127: 
  128: \begin{theoremB}[Affine fiber--partition correspondence]
  129: For every \(m\ge1\), the map \(\pi_m\) is a permutation and
  130: \[
  131:   d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))
  132:   =\widetilde d_m(n)
  133:   =R^\dagger(n)-R^\dagger(n-F_{m+1})
  134:   \qquad(0\le n<F_{m+2}).
  135: \]
  136: \end{theoremB}
  137: 
  138: This combines \Cref{thm:affine-transfer,thm:partition-difference}.  It gives a
  139: pointwise correspondence, not only an equality of aggregate moments.
  140: 
  141: There is also a direct interval description:
  142: \[
  143:  d_m^\#(r)=
  144:  \begin{cases}
  145:  R(F_{m+2}+r),&0\le r\le F_{m+1}-2,\\
  146:  R(r),&F_{m+1}-1\le r<F_{m+2}.
  147:  \end{cases}
  148: \]
  149: The known extremal classification of Koc\'abov\'a, Mas\'akov\'a, and
  150: Pelantov\'a can therefore be transferred exactly after an explicit change of
  151: Fibonacci convention.
  152: 
  153: \begin{theoremC}[Interval identity and largest fibers]
  154: Let \(D_m=\max_{x\in X_m}d_m(x)\).  Then
  155: \[
  156:  D_0=D_1=1,\qquad D_{2k}=F_{k+2}\ (k\ge0),\qquad
  157:  D_{2k+1}=2F_{k+1}\ (k\ge1),
  158: \]
  159: and \(D_m=D_{m-2}+D_{m-4}\) for every \(m\ge6\).  The maximizing residues,
  160: including the exceptional initial cases, are given in
  161: \Cref{thm:unconditional-extremal-fibers}.
  162: \end{theoremC}
  163: 
  164: For a real tilt \(t\), put
  165: \[
  166:   S_t(m)=\sum_{x\in X_m}d_m(x)^t.
  167: \]
  168: The level sets of \(R\) are sufficiently sparse to produce a frozen
  169: half-line.
  170: 
  171: \begin{theoremD}[Negative-temperature freezing]
  172: For every real \(t\le-\sigma_0\),
  173: \[
  174:   \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_t(m)=0.
  175: \]
  176: Consequently these pressure limits cannot be represented on all real tilts by
  177: the logarithm of a positive simple isolated eigenvalue of an analytic compact
  178: operator family on a fixed Banach space.
  179: \end{theoremD}
  180: 
  181: The freezing theorem certifies the half-line \(t\le-\sigma_0\).  The weighted
  182: renewal theorem below supplies the complementary positivity in the candidate
  183: strip and proves that \(-\sigma_0\) is the exact zero-pressure boundary.
  184: 
  185: The fixed-level information is sharper than the exponential pressure.  Put
  186: \(C_m(k)=\card{\{x\in X_m:d_m(x)=k\}}\), and let \(\Psi(k)\) be Weinstein's
  187: number of generating orbits at level \(k\).
  188: 
  189: \begin{theoremE}[Local spectrum and critical Gibbs law]
  190: For \(m\ge1\), \(C_m(1)=2\), while for \(k\ge2\),
  191: \[
  192:  C_m(k)\le4\Psi(k),
  193:  \qquad C_m(k)=4\Psi(k)\quad(m\ge2k).
  194: \]
  195: For every \(s>\sigma_0\),
  196: \[
  197:  S_{-s}(m)\longrightarrow
  198:  \mathcal Z(s):=\frac4{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}-2.
  199: \]
  200: This is the exact convergence range for the unnormalized sums.  The function
  201: \(\mathcal Z\) has a simple pole at \(\sigma_0\), and the corresponding
  202: fiber-level Gibbs measures converge in total variation with explicit
  203: critical logarithmic moments.
  204: At the endpoint itself,
  205: \[
  206:  S_{-\sigma_0}(m)\sim\frac{4m}{\mu_C}.
  207: \]
  208: Under the finite-window Gibbs law proportional to
  209: \(d_m(x)^{-\sigma_0}\), the normalized pair consisting of log multiplicity
  210: and generating-layer cost converges in distribution to
  211: \((v_cU,U)\), where \(U\) is uniform on \([0,1]\).
  212: \end{theoremE}
  213: 
  214: The complete statements are
  215: \Cref{thm:local-fiber-spectrum,thm:spectral-partition-function,thm:critical-gibbs-spectrum,thm:critical-finite-window-gibbs}.
  216: They separate an unnormalized critical phenomenon inside the region where
  217: the exponential pressure vanishes.
  218: 
  219: \begin{theoremF}[Pressure dictionary and critical refinements]
  220: With
  221: \[
  222:  \kappa=-\left.\frac{d}{ds}\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}
  223:  \right|_{s=\sigma_0},
  224: \]
  225: one has
  226: \[
  227:  \sum_{k\le x}\Psi(k)\sim
  228:  \frac{x^{\sigma_0}}{\sigma_0\kappa}.
  229: \]
  230: Moreover, if
  231: \(\beta_*=\log\varphi/\log(2/\varphi)=2.2705594539\ldots\), then
  232: \[
  233:  \liminf_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_t(m)>0
  234:  \qquad(t>-\beta_*).
  235: \]
  236: For every real \(t\), the limit \(P(t)\) exists.  If
  237: \(t>-\sigma_0\), it is positive and is determined by the
  238: weighted-generator equation
  239: \[
  240:  \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
  241:  q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}=1.
  242: \]
  243: For \(t\le-\sigma_0\), one has \(P(t)=0\).  On
  244: \((-\sigma_0,\infty)\), the pressure is real analytic and strictly convex.
  245: At \(t_c=-\sigma_0\), its left derivative is zero and its right derivative is
  246: a finite, explicitly convergent positive ratio of continued-fraction moments.
  247: \end{theoremF}
  248: 
  249: The all-real existence, implicit equation, frozen branch, and derivative
  250: corner in this statement are not claimed as new: through
  251: \eqref{eq:erdos-pressure-dictionary} below they recover the spectrum theorem
  252: of Feng \cite[Thm.~1.3]{Feng2005} and the Erd\H{o}s-measure phase transition of
  253: Feng--Olivier \cite[Thms.~2.9--2.10 and App.~A]{FengOlivier2003}.  The
